The fixed loci of the T‑action on M0,1(CP2,1) correspond to maps whose image is a T‑invariant line. There are three such lines, each contributing a term frac{1}{e_T(Nvir)}. The virtual normal bundle splits as a sum of line bundles with weights given by the torus characters. Evaluating the equivariant Euler class and summing over the three fixed loci yields langlept⟩0,1=1, which matches the classical count of lines through a point in CP2.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a localisation computation, first list all torus‑fixed components of the moduli space. Write the virtual normal bundle as a direct sum of line bundles with explicit weights. The contribution of each component is the integral of the pull‑back of the insertions divided by the equivariant Euler class of the virtual normal bundle. Sum the contributions; cancellations often simplify the final answer dramatically.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Monopoles and the Seiberg–Witten Equations

The Seiberg–Witten moduli space MSW(X,s) on a smooth closed oriented four‑manifold X with a spin^c structure s is the space of solutions (A,psi) to the Seiberg–Witten equations modulo gauge. The equations are F_A+ = q(psi) and D_Apsi=0, where q(psi) is a quadratic map from the spinor bundle to self‑dual two‑forms. The deformation complex is an elliptic complex whose index gives the expected dimension d = (1)/(4)(c1(s)2 - 2chi(X) - 3σ(X)). The L2 metric on the tangent space H1 is defined by integrating the pointwise inner product of Lie‑algebra valued forms and spinors. The resulting metric is hyperkähler when X admits a hyperkähler structure; otherwise it is a Riemannian metric with a natural almost complex structure.

The Seiberg–Witten invariants are defined by integrating cohomology classes on the compactified moduli space against the fundamental class. The μ‑map sends a homology class Σ∈ H2(X) to a cohomology class μ(Σ)∈ H2(MSW) via the universal bundle. The basic classes are those spin^c structures for which the invariant is non‑zero. The wall‑crossing formula describes how the invariant changes when the metric crosses a wall in the space of metrics; the change is given by an integral of a differential form over the moduli space of reducible solutions, which is a copy of the Jacobian torus H1(X;R)/H1(X;Z). The Seiberg–Witten equations also admit a three‑dimensional analogue on a three‑manifold Y, where the moduli space of solutions to the Chern–Simons–Dirac equations carries a natural symplectic form given by the L2 pairing of tangent vectors.

In the context of gauge theory on manifolds with boundary, the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for the Dirac operator with spectral boundary conditions yields a formula for the index in terms of the eta‑invariant of the boundary operator. The eta‑invariant is expressed as an integral of a differential form on the boundary, the Chern–Simons form of the induced connection. This relation underlies the definition of the Seiberg–Witten invariants for four‑manifolds with boundary and the gluing formula for the invariants under cutting along a three‑manifold. The monopole Floer homology of a three‑manifold is constructed from the Chern–Simons–Dirac functional; its chain complex is generated by critical points (solutions to the three‑dimensional Seiberg–Witten equations) and the differential counts flow lines, which are solutions on R× Y. The resulting homology groups are invariants of Y and are isomorphic to the Heegaard Floer homology.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Flat Connections on Three‑Manifolds and Chern–Simons Theory

For a compact three‑manifold Y and a compact Lie group G, the moduli space Mflat(Y,G) of flat G‑connections modulo gauge is a finite‑dimensional real algebraic variety. Its tangent space at a flat connection A is the first cohomology H1(Y;g_A) of the twisted de Rham complex. The Atiyah–Bott symplectic form on Mflat(Y,G) is given by ω(α,β)=∫_Y ⟨αwedgeβ⟩ for α,β∈ H1, where ⟨·,·⟩ is the Killing form. This symplectic form is the curvature of the prequantum line bundle over the space of all connections, and its restriction to the flat locus makes Mflat(Y,G) a symplectic manifold (possibly with singularities at reducible connections).

Chern–Simons theory assigns to Y a topological quantum field theory whose partition function is the path integral ∫ exp((ik)/(4π) SCS(A)) DA with SCS(A)=∫_Y Tr(Awedge dA+(2)/(3)Awedge Awedge A). The classical equations of motion are the flatness condition F_A=0. The perturbative expansion around a flat connection A0 yields Feynman diagrams whose weights are integrals of differential forms on configuration spaces of points in Y. The resulting invariants are the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants, which can be computed via surgery presentations and the modular tensor category of representations of the quantum group U_q(g) at a root of unity. The asymptotic expansion of the Chern–Simons partition function as k→∞ is governed by the complex Chern–Simons invariant of the flat connections, and the leading term is the Ray–Singer analytic torsion of the twisted de Rham complex.

The moduli space of flat connections on a surface Σ appears as the phase space of Chern–Simons theory on ΣtimesR. The Goldman bracket on functions on Mflat(Σ,G) is induced by the Atiyah–Bott symplectic form and makes the algebra of functions a Poisson algebra. Quantisation of this Poisson algebra yields the skein algebra of the surface, which is isomorphic to the algebra of Wilson loop operators in Chern–Simons theory. The mapping class group of Σ acts by symplectomorphisms on Mflat(Σ,G), and its projective representation on the space of conformal blocks of the associated WZW model is the monodromy of the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov connection, a flat connection on the bundle of conformal blocks over the moduli space of curves. The differential forms representing the KZ connection are built from the universal Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov form on the configuration space of points on Σ.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Logarithmic Connections on Algebraic Varieties

Let X be a smooth projective variety over C and D⊂ X a simple normal crossing divisor. A logarithmic connection on a vector bundle E is a connection ∇:E→ E⊗Ω1_X(log D) with poles of order at most one along D and residues satisfying the integrability condition ∇wedge∇=0. The moduli space Mlog(X,D) of logarithmic connections with fixed residues is a quasi‑projective variety carrying a natural holomorphic symplectic form Ω obtained by the Atiyah–Bott construction: for tangent vectors represented by logarithmic forms α,β∈ H1(X,operatorname{End}E⊗Ω1_X(log D)), set Ω(α,β)=∫_X Tr(αwedgeβ). This form is non‑degenerate on the smooth locus and makes Mlog(X,D) a holomorphic symplectic manifold.

The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Deligne and Malgrange identifies the moduli space of logarithmic connections with the moduli space of meromorphic flat connections with prescribed formal types at the divisor. The Stokes data at the irregular singularities are encoded by a collection of Stokes matrices, which vary holomorphically with the connection. The isomonodromic deformation equations (the Schlesinger system for Fuchsian systems, and the Jimbo–Miwa–Ueno system for irregular systems) describe the evolution of the connection under deformation of the divisor or the residues. These equations are Hamiltonian flows with respect to the holomorphic symplectic form Ω, and the Hamiltonians are given by residues of the connection at the singularities. The tau‑function of the isomonodromic system is defined as τ = exp∫ ωtau, where ωtau is a closed one‑form on the deformation space constructed from the residue of the connection.

In the geometric Langlands program, the moduli space of logarithmic G‑connections on a curve C with ramification at marked points is the spectral side of the correspondence. The Hecke eigensheaf property translates into the condition that the D‑module on the moduli stack of G‑bundles is an eigenobject for the Hecke operators. The differential forms on the moduli space of logarithmic connections provide the geometric realisation of the Hecke operators via the convolution diagram. The categorical Langlands correspondence predicts an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on the moduli stack of G‑bundles and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of ^L G‑local systems, where ^L G is the Langlands dual group. The Fourier–Mukai kernel implementing this equivalence is built from the universal logarithmic connection and its associated differential forms on the product of the two moduli stacks.

  • Confusing the virtual fundamental class of the stable‑map moduli space with the ordinary fundamental class; the former lives in the Chow group of the stack and requires a perfect obstruction theory.
  • Assuming the Donaldson invariants are metric‑independent for all four‑manifolds; they are invariant only for manifolds with b2+>1 and change according to the wall‑crossing formula when b2+=1.
  • Treating the Seiberg–Witten basic classes as characteristic classes of the tangent bundle; they are spin^c structures whose associated line bundle has non‑zero Seiberg–Witten invariant.
  • Neglecting the contribution of reducible flat connections in the Chern–Simons path integral; these give rise to singularities in the moduli space and require a careful choice of framing and regularisation.
  • Identifying the holomorphic symplectic form on the logarithmic connection moduli space with the real symplectic form from the Atiyah–Bott construction; they differ by a factor of i and correspond to different complex structures in the hyperkähler triple.
  • Overlooking the role of the Stokes matrices in the irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence; the monodromy data alone does not determine the connection without the Stokes data.
  • Assuming the Goldman bracket on the character variety of a surface is the same as the Poisson bracket from the Atiyah–Bott form on the moduli space of flat connections; they coincide only after identifying the character variety with the smooth locus of the moduli space.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Pseudoholomorphic Curves

The moduli space of pseudoholomorphic curves in a symplectic manifold (M, ω) equipped with an ω‑compatible almost complex structure J is the central object of Gromov–Witten theory. A pseudoholomorphic curve is a map u: Σ → M from a Riemann surface Σ such that du ∘ j = J ∘ du, where j is the complex structure on Σ. The linearisation of this equation at a solution u is a Cauchy–Riemann type operator D_u: Γ(u*TM) → Γ(Λ0,1Σ ⊗ u*TM). Its Fredholm index is given by the Riemann–Roch formula ind(D_u) = (n−3)(2−2g) + 2c1(TM)[u_*[Σ]], where n = dim_ℝ M / 2 and g is the genus of Σ. The moduli space ℳg,k(M, A) of genus g curves with k marked points representing homology class A carries a virtual fundamental class [ℳ]vir ∈ H2 ind(D_u)(ℳ; ℚ) constructed via Kuranishi structures or polyfold theory. Differential forms on ℳ arise from the universal curve π: 𝒞 → ℳ and the evaluation maps ev_i: ℳ → M. The pullback ev_i^*α of a cohomology class α ∈ H^*(M) yields a form on ℳ, and integration over the virtual class defines Gromov–Witten invariants ⟨τd1α1 … τd_kα_k⟩g,A = ∫[ℳ]vir ∏ ψ_id_i ∧ ev_i^*α_i, where ψ_i are the first Chern classes of the cotangent line bundles at the marked points. The axioms of a cohomological field theory (splitting, gluing, and divisor equations) are proved by analysing the boundary strata of the compactified moduli space, which correspond to stable maps with nodal domains. The differential forms representing the ψ‑classes are constructed from the universal cotangent line bundle and satisfy the string and dilaton equations. In the algebraic category, the same invariants are defined via the virtual localisation formula of Graber–Pandharipande, where the torus action on M lifts to ℳ and the fixed loci are described by decorated graphs. The resulting expressions involve integrals over products of moduli spaces of stable curves, linking Gromov–Witten theory to tautological intersection theory on ℳg,k. Examiners often test the distinction between the naive fundamental class (which exists only when ℳ is smooth of expected dimension) and the virtual class (which exists for all (M, ω, J)). A common trap is to assume that the ψ‑classes are pulled back from the moduli space of curves ℳg,k without the evaluation maps; in fact ψ_i = π_*(ω_π) where ω_π is the relative dualising sheaf of the universal curve, and its definition depends on the stabilisation of the domain.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Harmonic Maps

Let (Σ, h) be a compact Riemann surface and (N, g) a compact Riemannian manifold. A map φ: Σ → N is harmonic if it is a critical point of the Dirichlet energy E(φ) = ½ ∫_Σ |dφ|2 dvol_h. The Euler–Lagrange equation is τ(φ) = trace_h ∇dφ = 0, where τ(φ) is the tension field. The moduli space ℋ(Σ, N) of harmonic maps modulo the action of the diffeomorphism group of Σ (when Σ has genus ≥ 2) carries a natural L2 metric induced from the H1 Sobolev space. The tangent space at φ is the kernel of the Jacobi operator J_φ = Δ_φ + R^N(dφ, ·)dφ, an elliptic self‑adjoint operator on sections of φ*TN. The index of J_φ equals the Morse index of φ as a critical point of E. When N has non‑positive sectional curvature, E is convex and ℋ consists of a single point (the constant map) or is empty; for positively curved targets (e.g., spheres) the moduli space can have multiple components. The differential forms on ℋ are constructed via the universal map Φ: Σ × ℋ → N and the projection π: Σ × ℋ → ℋ. For any invariant polynomial P on the Lie algebra of the isometry group of N, the form π_*(P(F_Φ)) where F_Φ is the curvature of the pullback connection on Φ*TN yields a closed form on ℋ. In the case N = S2, the energy identity of Sacks–Uhlenbeck shows that sequences of harmonic maps with bounded energy converge away from a finite set of points where energy concentrates; the limiting object is a harmonic map from a nodal Riemann surface (a “bubble tree”). The compactified moduli space carries a virtual fundamental class analogous to the Gromov–Witten case, and integration of the pulled‑back forms gives invariants of the pair (Σ, N). The heat flow method of Eells–Sampson provides a deformation retraction of the mapping space onto ℋ when N has non‑positive curvature, yielding a homotopy equivalence. For general N, the Palais–Smale condition fails, and the topology of ℋ is studied via the Morse theory of E on the loop space of N. Examiners frequently ask for the explicit formula of the Jacobi operator in local coordinates: (J_φ V)^α = Δ V^α + R^αβγδ ∂_i φ^β ∂^i φ^γ V^δ, where Δ is the rough Laplacian on Σ. A typical trap is to confuse the tension field τ(φ) with the Laplacian of φ; τ(φ) = Δ φ + Γ^αβγ ∂_i φ^β ∂^i φ^γ, where Γ are the Christoffel symbols of N.

Technique: Virtual Fundamental Class vs. Ordinary Fundamental Class
Let ℳ be the moduli space of stable maps of genus 0 with 3 marked points to ℙ2 representing the class of a line. The expected dimension is 2·3 + (3−3)(1−0) = 6. Explain why ℳ is not a smooth manifold of dimension 6 and how the virtual fundamental class [ℳ]vir is used to define the Gromov–Witten invariant ⟨pt, pt, pt⟩0, [line].
The moduli space ℳ parametrises maps u: ℙ1 → ℙ2 of degree 1 with three marked points. Such a map is an isomorphism onto a line in ℙ2, and the three marked points can be any three distinct points on that line. The space of lines in ℙ2 is ℙ2* ≅ ℙ2 (dimension 2). For each line, the choice of three ordered distinct points gives an open subset of (ℙ1)3 of dimension 3. Thus the naive dimension of ℳ is 2 + 3 = 5, not 6. The discrepancy arises because the linearised Cauchy–Riemann operator at a degree‑1 map has index 6, but the automorphism group of ℙ1 (PGL(2, ℂ), dimension 3) acts on the domain, reducing the actual dimension by 3. The virtual fundamental class is constructed by choosing a perfect obstruction theory E^• → L_ℳ, where E^• = [T_ℳ → Ob] with Ob the obstruction sheaf (here zero because the target is convex). The virtual class [ℳ]vir ∈ A6(ℳ) is then the top Chern class of the obstruction bundle, which in this case equals the ordinary fundamental class of the 5‑dimensional space pushed forward to dimension 6 via the inclusion of the boundary strata. The invariant ⟨pt, pt, pt⟩0, [line] is computed by evaluating the three evaluation maps ev_i: ℳ → ℙ2 at the point class pt ∈ H4(ℙ2). Since three generic points in ℙ2 determine a unique line, the integral ∫[ℳ]vir ev1^*pt ∧ ev2^*pt ∧ ev3^*pt equals 1. This matches the classical fact that there is exactly one line through three generic points in ℙ2.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a Gromov–Witten invariant, first compute the expected dimension using the Riemann–Roch formula. If the actual moduli space has lower dimension, recall that the virtual class lives in the expected dimension and is defined via a perfect obstruction theory. Write down the evaluation maps and the ψ‑classes explicitly; the string and dilaton equations often reduce the number of integrals needed. For harmonic map questions, identify the tension field and the Jacobi operator; the Morse index equals the number of negative eigenvalues of the Jacobi operator. Remember that energy concentration (bubbling) occurs only when the target has positive curvature.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Flat Connections on Higher Dimensional Manifolds

Let X be a closed oriented manifold of dimension d ≥ 4 and G a compact Lie group. The moduli space ℳflat(X, G) of flat G‑connections modulo gauge equivalence is the space of representations π1(X) → G modulo conjugation. When d = 4, the Atiyah–Bott symplectic form on the space of connections descends to a symplectic structure on the smooth part of ℳflat(X, G) via the identification T[A]ℳ ≅ H1(X; ad A). The differential forms on ℳ are generated by the universal connection 𝒜 on the bundle 𝒫 → X × ℳ and the characteristic forms Tr(F_𝒜^k). Since F_𝒜 = 0 on the flat locus, the non‑trivial forms come from the transgression of the Chern–Simons form: for a path of connections A_t, the Chern–Simons form CS(A0, A1) = ∫01 Tr(ȧ_t ∧ FA_t) dt yields a closed form on ℳ when integrated over cycles in X. In dimension d = 3, the Chern–Simons functional CS(A) = ∫_X Tr(A ∧ dA + ⅔ A ∧ A ∧ A) defines a circle‑valued function on the space of connections; its critical points are flat connections, and the Hessian at a flat connection is the operator *d_A on Ω1(X; ad A). The resulting symplectic form on ℳflat(X, G) is the Atiyah–Bott form ω(α, β) = ∫_X Tr(α ∧ β) for α, β ∈ H1(X; ad A). For d ≥ 5, the deformation complex of a flat connection is 0 → Ω0(ad A) → Ω1(ad A) → Ω2(ad A) → …, and the expected dimension of ℳ is given by the Euler characteristic χ(X; ad A) = Σ (−1)^i dim H^i(X; ad A). The virtual fundamental class is constructed from the Kuranishi map κ: H1 → H2, and the differential forms on the virtual neighbourhood are obtained by pulling back the universal characteristic forms via the Kuranishi chart. In the case X = Σ × S1 with Σ a surface, ℳflat(X, G) is identified with the moduli space of Higgs bundles on Σ via the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence; the differential forms then match the hyperkähler forms on the Hitchin moduli space. Examiners often test the relation between the Chern–Simons invariant and the eta invariant of the odd‑signature operator on X, as in the work of Atiyah–Patodi–Singer. A common mistake is to assume that the symplectic form on ℳflat(X, G) for d = 4 is the same as the one for d = 3; they differ by a factor of the volume of the extra dimension and correspond to different complex structures in the hyperkähler triple when X admits a hyperkähler metric.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Vortices on Riemann Surfaces

Let Σ be a compact Riemann surface of genus g, L a holomorphic line bundle of degree d, and consider the vortex equations for a pair (A, φ) where A is a unitary connection on L and φ ∈ Ω0(Σ, L) is a section: F_A + i( |φ|2 − τ ) ω_Σ = 0, ∂̄_A φ = 0. Here τ > 0 is a real parameter (the vortex number) and ω_Σ is the Kähler form. The moduli space 𝒱τ,d(Σ) of solutions modulo gauge equivalence is a smooth Kähler manifold of complex dimension d when τ > 2πd / Vol(Σ) (Bradlow’s bound). The vortex equations are the dimensional reduction of the Yang–Mills–Higgs equations on Σ × ℝ2, and the moduli space carries a natural symplectic form ω_𝒱 = ∫_Σ Tr(δA ∧ δA) + i ∫_Σ (δφ̄ ∧ δφ) ω_Σ. The differential forms on 𝒱 are generated by the universal vortex (𝒜, Φ) over Σ × 𝒱. The first Chern class of the determinant line bundle Det(∂̄_𝒜) yields a form c1(Det) = i ∫_Σ Tr(F_𝒜) which coincides with the Kähler form ω_𝒱. The integration of ω_𝒱^k over 𝒱 gives the volume, which by the work of García‑Prada and Mundet i Riera equals the symplectic volume of the moduli space of parabolic bundles on Σ with weights determined by τ. When τ approaches the Bradlow bound, the vortices concentrate at points and the moduli space compactifies to the symmetric product Sym^d(Σ); the limiting differential forms are the tautological classes on Sym^d(Σ). The vortex moduli space also appears as the fixed point set of a circle action on the moduli space of Higgs bundles (the Hitchin system), and the localisation formula expresses integrals over the Higgs bundle moduli space in terms of integrals over 𝒱. Examiners may ask for the explicit expression of the moment map for the U(1) action rotating the phase of φ: μ(A, φ) = ∫_Σ ( |φ|2 − τ ) ω_Σ. A typical trap is to confuse the vortex number τ with the degree d; the existence condition is τ > 2πd / Vol(Σ), not τ = d.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations

The Kapustin–Witten equations are a family of partial differential equations on a four‑manifold M parameterised by t ∈ ℂℙ1, arising from the topological twist of N = 4 super Yang–Mills theory. For a principal G‑bundle P → M, a solution is a pair (A, φ) where A is a connection on P and φ ∈ Ω1(M, ad P) satisfies F_A − φ ∧ φ + t d_A φ = 0 and d_A * φ = 0. When t = 0 the equations reduce to the anti‑self‑duality equations F_A+ = 0 and d_A φ = 0, whose moduli space is the instanton moduli space. When t = 1 they become the Haydys–Witten equations, related to the geometric Langlands program. The moduli space ℳKW(M, G, t) carries a natural hyperkähler structure for generic t, with three complex structures I, J, K satisfying the quaternion relations. The differential forms on ℳ are generated by the universal fields (𝒜, Φ) over M × ℳ. The hyperkähler forms ω_I, ω_J, ω_K are given by ω_I = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δA + δφ ∧ δφ), ω_J = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δφ), ω_K = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ *δφ). These forms are closed and satisfy the hyperkähler identities. The work of Gaiotto–Moore–Neitzke relates the integrals of these forms over cycles in ℳ to the BPS spectrum of the associated quantum field theory. In the case M = Σ × C with Σ a Riemann surface and C a cylinder, the moduli space is identified with the moduli space of Higgs bundles on Σ, and the Kapustin–Witten equations become Hitchin’s equations. The parameter t corresponds to the complex structure on the Hitchin base. The differential forms on ℳ then match the tautological forms on the Hitchin moduli space, and the S‑duality of the gauge theory predicts a mirror symmetry between ℳKW(M, G, t) and ℳKW(M, ^L G, −1/t). Examiners often test the identification of the three complex structures: I is the complex structure coming from the complex structure on M, J is the one from the complex structure on the space of connections, and K = IJ. A common error is to treat the parameter t as a real coupling constant; it is a point on ℂℙ1 and the equations are elliptic only for t ∈ ℝ ∪ iℝ.

  • Confusing the virtual fundamental class of the pseudoholomorphic curve moduli space with the ordinary fundamental class; the former lives in the expected dimension and requires a perfect obstruction theory.
  • Assuming the Jacobi operator for harmonic maps is simply the Laplacian on sections of φ*TN; it includes the curvature term R^N(dφ, ·)dφ.
  • Treating the symplectic form on the flat connection moduli space in dimension 4 as identical to the Atiyah–Bott form in dimension 3; they differ by a volume factor and correspond to different complex structures.
  • Identifying the vortex parameter τ with the degree d of the line bundle; the existence condition is τ > 2πd / Vol(Σ).
  • Regarding the Kapustin–Witten parameter t as a real coupling constant rather than a point on ℂℙ1; ellipticity holds only for t ∈ ℝ ∪ iℝ.
  • Neglecting the role of the Kuranishi map in constructing the virtual class for higher‑dimensional flat connections; the obstruction space is H2(X; ad A) and the virtual dimension is χ(X; ad A).
  • Review these traps carefully before the exam.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations

The Vafa–Witten equations arise from the topological twist of N = 4 super Yang–Mills theory on a closed oriented four‑manifold X. For a principal G‑bundle P → X, a solution is a pair (A, B) where A is a connection on P and B ∈ Ω2,+(X, ad P) is a self‑dual two‑form with values in the adjoint bundle. The equations read FA+ = [B, B] and dAB = 0, together with the Bianchi identity dA*B = 0. The moduli space ℳVW(X, G) carries a natural virtual fundamental class constructed via the obstruction theory of the elliptic complex Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) ⊕ Ω2,+(ad P) → Ω2,+(ad P) ⊕ Ω3(ad P). Differential forms on ℳ are generated by the universal fields (𝒜, ℬ) over X × ℳ. The basic observables are the integrals ∫X Tr(ℱ𝒜 ∧ ℱ𝒜) and ∫X Tr(ℬ ∧ ℬ), which descend to cohomology classes on ℳ. Vafa and Witten showed that the partition function of the twisted theory computes the Euler characteristics of instanton moduli spaces and is a modular form. The S‑duality of the physical theory predicts an isomorphism between ℳVW(X, G) and ℳVW(X, LG) where LG is the Langlands dual group. Examiners often test the identification of the virtual dimension: vdim ℳ = 2∫X c2(ad P) − (dim G)(χ(X) + σ(X))/2. A common trap is to confuse the Vafa–Witten equations with the Kapustin–Witten equations at t = 0; the former involve a self‑dual two‑form B while the latter involve a one‑form φ.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Calabi–Yau Threefolds

Donaldson–Thomas (DT) theory counts ideal sheaves (or equivalently, subschemes) on a Calabi–Yau threefold Y with a fixed Chern character. The moduli space ℳDT(Y, v) of stable objects in the derived category Db(Coh Y) carries a symmetric obstruction theory, yielding a virtual fundamental class [M]vir in the expected dimension zero. Differential forms on ℳ are not ordinary forms but rather elements of the derived de Rham complex; the virtual class is represented by the Behrend function ν: ℳ → ℤ, and the DT invariant is ∫[ℳ]vir 1 = ∑p∈ℳ ν(p). The Joyce–Song wall‑crossing formula expresses how DT invariants change under variation of stability condition; it involves the motivic Hall algebra and the integration map to the quantum torus. The generating series of DT invariants is conjecturally equal to the topological string partition function (the MNOP conjecture). In the local case Y = Tot(KS) for a surface S, the DT invariants reduce to the Vafa–Witten invariants of S. Examiners may ask for the definition of the virtual tangent bundle: Tvir = RHom(E, E)0[1] where E is the universal complex. A typical error is to treat the DT moduli space as a smooth manifold; it is a derived scheme with a perfect obstruction theory, and its tangent‑obstruction complex is Ext1(E, E) → Ext2(E, E).

Worked Example: Virtual Dimension of a DT Moduli Space
Let Y be a Calabi–Yau threefold and E a stable sheaf with Chern character ch(E) = (0, 0, β, n) where β ∈ H2(Y, ℤ) and n ∈ ℤ. Compute the virtual dimension of the DT moduli space at [E].
The virtual dimension is given by the Euler characteristic of the deformation‑obstruction complex: vdim = χ(Y, Rℋ𝑜𝓂(E, E)0). By Serre duality on a Calabi–Yau threefold, Ext3(E, E) ≅ Hom(E, E)* and Ext2(E, E) ≅ Ext1(E, E)*. The trace‑free condition removes the trivial summand from Hom(E, E) ≅ ℂ. Hence χ = dim Ext1 − dim Ext2 = 0. Therefore the virtual dimension is zero, consistent with the fact that DT invariants are numbers.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the virtual dimension of a DT moduli space, immediately write the formula vdim = ∫Y ch(E)∨ ch(E) td(Y) and simplify using Calabi–Yau conditions (c1 = 0, td = 1 + c2/12 + …). The answer is often zero for numerical invariants; if the problem asks for the dimension of the actual moduli space (not virtual), recall that it can be positive and is given by dim Ext1(E, E). Distinguish these two clearly.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Strominger System on Heterotic String Backgrounds

The Strominger system describes supersymmetric vacua of the heterotic string on a six‑dimensional manifold X with a holomorphic vector bundle V → X. The fields are a Hermitian metric ω on X, a connection A on V satisfying the Hermitian Yang–Mills equations FA0,2 = 0, Λω FA = 0, and a three‑form H = dcω ω + (α′/4)(CS(A) − CS(θ)) where θ is the Chern connection of ω and CS denotes the Chern–Simons form. The Bianchi identity dH = (α′/4)(Tr FA ∧ FA − Tr Rθ ∧ Rθ) encodes anomaly cancellation. The moduli space ℳStr of solutions modulo gauge transformations carries a natural symplectic form derived from the kinetic terms of the effective action. Differential forms on ℳ are generated by the universal fields (𝒲, 𝒜, ℋ) over X × ℳ. The infinitesimal deformations are governed by a differential complex combining the Dolbeault complex of X with the adjoint bundle of V and the tangent bundle of X. The virtual dimension is given by the index of this complex, which equals ∫X (ch(V) − ch(TX)) td(X). A key result of Li–Yau and Garcia‑Fernandez is that the moduli space admits a generalized Kähler structure. Examiners often test the role of the Chern–Simons correction in H: it ensures that dH matches the anomaly polynomial. A common mistake is to omit the Chern–Simons term for the tangent bundle, leading to an incorrect Bianchi identity.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Generalized Self‑Duality Equations on G₂ Manifolds

On a seven‑dimensional manifold M with a G₂‑structure defined by a positive three‑form φ, the generalized self‑duality equations for a connection A on a principal G‑bundle are *φ FA = − FA ∧ φ. Equivalently, the curvature lies in the 14‑dimensional irreducible representation of G₂ inside Λ2 ⊗ ad P. The moduli space ℳG₂(M, G) of solutions modulo gauge transformations is expected to be a finite‑dimensional manifold (or orbifold) when M is compact and the G₂‑structure is torsion‑free. The deformation complex is Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) → Ω214(ad P), where the last map is the projection of dA onto the 14 component. Its index gives the virtual dimension: vdim = −∫M (p1(ad P) ∧ φ + …). Differential forms on ℳ are generated by the universal curvature ℱ𝒜 and the universal G₂‑form Φ. The natural symplectic form on ℳ is ω = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ *Φ δ𝒜). Donaldson and Segal proposed a program to define invariants of G₂ manifolds by counting solutions to these equations, analogous to Donaldson theory for four‑manifolds. The equations also appear as the dimensional reduction of the octonionic instanton equations on Spin(7) eight‑manifolds. Examiners may ask for the linearisation of the G₂‑instanton equation: *φ dA a = − dA a ∧ φ. A frequent trap is to confuse the 14‑dimensional projection with the 7‑dimensional one; the latter corresponds to the torsion of the G₂‑structure and vanishes for torsion‑free structures.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations with Surface Operators

Surface operators in the Kapustin–Witten theory are supported on a smoothly embedded surface Σ ⊂ M and prescribe a singularity of the fields along Σ. For a principal G‑bundle P → M Σ with a prescribed monodromy around Σ, a solution consists of a connection A and a one‑form φ satisfying the Kapustin–Witten equations away from Σ, with the boundary condition that A has a logarithmic pole and φ has a prescribed residue along Σ. The moduli space ℳKW,Σ(M, G, t) carries a hyperkähler structure for generic t ∈ ℂℙ1. The differential forms on ℳ are generated by the universal fields (𝒜, Φ) over (M Σ) × ℳ together with the universal monodromy data on Σ. The hyperkähler forms acquire additional contributions from the surface: ωI = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δ𝒜 + δΦ ∧ δΦ) + ∫Σ Tr(δ𝒜Σ ∧ δΦΣ). The work of Gaiotto–Witten and Kapustin–Witten relates the category of boundary conditions for surface operators to the geometric Langlands correspondence with ramification. The parameter t controls the complex structure on the Hitchin base when M = Σ × C. Examiners often test the identification of the residue of φ as the Higgs field on Σ in the Hitchin system. A common error is to treat the surface operator as a simple defect without the required monodromy data; the moduli space is empty unless the monodromy matches the prescribed conjugacy class.

  • Confusing the Vafa–Witten equations with the Kapustin–Witten equations at t = 0; the former involve a self‑dual two‑form B, the latter a one‑form φ.
  • Treating the Donaldson–Thomas moduli space as a smooth manifold; it is a derived scheme with a perfect obstruction theory and its tangent‑obstruction complex is Ext1 → Ext2.
  • Omitting the Chern–Simons correction for the tangent bundle in the Strominger system Bianchi identity; the correct identity is dH = (α′/4)(Tr FA ∧ FA − Tr Rθ ∧ Rθ).
  • Mixing the 14‑dimensional and 7‑dimensional projections in the G₂‑instanton equation; only the 14 component appears for torsion‑free G₂ structures.
  • Ignoring the monodromy data when analysing surface operators in the Kapustin–Witten equations; the moduli space is empty unless the monodromy matches the prescribed conjugacy class.
  • Assuming the virtual dimension of the DT moduli space equals the actual dimension; the virtual dimension is zero for numerical invariants, while the actual dimension can be positive.
  • Misidentifying the parameter t in the Kapustin–Witten equations as a real coupling constant; it is a point on ℂℙ1 and ellipticity holds only for t ∈ ℝ ∪ iℝ.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Hermitian Yang‑Mills Equations on Calabi‑Yau Manifolds

The Hermitian Yang‑Mills (HYM) equations on a compact Kähler manifold X of complex dimension n with Kähler form ω read FA0,2 = 0 and ΛωFA = λ·Id, where FA is the curvature of a unitary connection A on a holomorphic vector bundle E, Λω denotes contraction with ω, and λ = 2πμ(E)/Vol(X) with μ the slope. The Donaldson‑Uhlenbeck‑Yau theorem (1985‑86) establishes that a polystable holomorphic bundle admits a unique HYM connection, and conversely any HYM connection yields a polystable bundle. The moduli space MHYM(X,E) of gauge‑equivalence classes of irreducible HYM connections carries a natural Kähler structure induced from the L2 metric on the space of connections. Its tangent space at [A] is the first cohomology of the elliptic deformation complex Ω0(ad E) → Ω0,1(ad E) ⊕ Ω1,0(ad E) → Ω1,1(ad E)0, where the subscript 0 denotes the primitive part. The virtual dimension is given by the index of this complex, which by the Atiyah‑Singer index theorem equals ∫X ch(ad E)·Td(X) evaluated on the fundamental class. For a Calabi‑Yau threefold with c1(X) = 0, the Todd class simplifies and the dimension formula reduces to −∫X (ch2(ad E)·ω + …). Differential forms on MHYM are generated by the universal curvature ℱ over X × MHYM; the Kähler form is ωM = ∫X Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ * δ𝒜) where * is the Hodge star on X. The HYM equations also appear as the dimensional reduction of the ten‑dimensional heterotic string equations, and the moduli space plays a central role in the construction of N=1 supersymmetric vacua. Examiners frequently test the identification of the stability condition with the existence of a HYM metric, the computation of the virtual dimension via the index theorem, and the fact that the moduli space is a quasi‑projective variety (by the work of Simpson). A common trap is to confuse the HYM equations with the ordinary Yang‑Mills equations; the former require the (0,2) part of the curvature to vanish, which is a first‑order condition, whereas Yang‑Mills is second‑order.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Bogomolny Equations on Three‑Manifolds

On an oriented Riemannian three‑manifold M with a principal G‑bundle P, the Bogomolny equations for a pair (A, Φ) consisting of a connection A and an adjoint‑valued Higgs field Φ are *FA = dAΦ, where * is the Hodge star on M. These equations describe static magnetic monopoles in three‑dimensional Yang‑Mills‑Higgs theory and are the dimensional reduction of the four‑dimensional self‑dual Yang‑Mills equations. The moduli space MBog(M,G) of solutions modulo gauge transformations is expected to be a smooth hyperkähler manifold when M = ℝ3 and appropriate boundary conditions (e.g., Φ → Φ∞ with |Φ∞| = 1) are imposed. The deformation complex is Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) ⊕ Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) ⊕ Ω0(ad P), where the first map is dA ⊕ [Φ,·] and the second is (*dA − [Φ,·]) ⊕ dA. Its index gives the virtual dimension; for G = SU(2) and charge k the dimension is 4k. The natural hyperkähler metric on MBog is obtained by the L2 inner product on the tangent space, and the three Kähler forms are ωI = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δ𝒜 + δΦ ∧ δΦ), ωJ = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δΦ − δΦ ∧ δ𝒜), ωK = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ * δΦ + δΦ ∧ * δ𝒜). The work of Taubes (1982) and Donaldson (1984) established the existence and compactness of monopole moduli spaces on ℝ3. For compact M with boundary, one imposes Nahm‑pole type boundary conditions, leading to a moduli space with a natural symplectic structure related to the Chern‑Simons functional on the boundary. Examiners often ask for the linearisation of the Bogomolny equations, the identification of the hyperkähler forms, and the computation of the dimension via the index theorem. A frequent error is to treat the Bogomolny equations as the same as the Hitchin equations on a Riemann surface; the latter involve a Higgs field valued in the canonical bundle, while the former uses an adjoint‑valued scalar on a three‑manifold.

Deformation Complex Computation
Let X be a Calabi‑Yau threefold with Kähler form ω and let E be a rank‑2 holomorphic vector bundle with c1(E) = 0 and c2(E) = 5. Compute the virtual dimension of the moduli space of irreducible Hermitian Yang‑Mills connections on E.
The deformation complex for HYM connections is Ω0(ad E) → Ω0,1(ad E) ⊕ Ω1,0(ad E) → Ω1,1(ad E)0. Its index equals ∫X ch(ad E)·Td(X). For a Calabi‑Yau threefold, Td(X) = 1 + ½c2(X) + … and ch(ad E) = rank(ad E) + ch1 + ch2 + ch3. Since c1(E) = 0, ch1(ad E) = 0. The rank of ad E is 3. The relevant term for the index in dimension six is the degree‑6 component of ch(ad E)·Td(X). Using ch2(ad E) = 2c2(E) − ½c1(E)2 = 2·5 = 10, and ch3(ad E) = 0 for rank‑2 bundle with c1=0. The integral reduces to ∫X (ch3(ad E) + ch2(ad E)·½c2(X)). Since ch3=0, the index is ½∫X ch2(ad E)·c2(X). For a generic Calabi‑Yau threefold, ∫X c2(X)·ω is a positive integer; however the virtual dimension formula for HYM on a threefold simplifies to −∫X ch2(ad E)·ω = −10·∫X ω∧ω∧ω/6. In many textbook examples the volume is normalized so that ∫X ω3 = 6, giving virtual dimension −10. The negative sign indicates that the expected dimension of the moduli space is zero and the obstruction space has dimension 10; the actual moduli space is a point (the unique HYM connection) when the bundle is stable.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the virtual dimension of a moduli space defined by first‑order elliptic equations (HYM, Bogomolny, instanton), write down the deformation complex immediately. Identify the symbol sequence, apply the Atiyah‑Singer index theorem, and reduce the characteristic class integral using the given topological data (Chern classes, Kähler form). Do not attempt to solve the PDE; the index computation is purely topological and yields full marks if the characteristic classes are correctly substituted.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Self‑Dual Yang‑Mills Equations on Four‑Manifolds

The self‑dual Yang‑Mills (SDYM) equations on an oriented Riemannian four‑manifold M are *FA = FA, where * is the Hodge star on two‑forms. Solutions are called instantons. The moduli space Mk(M,G) of gauge‑equivalence classes of irreducible G‑instantons with instanton number k = −¼∫M Tr(FA∧FA) carries a natural hyperkähler structure when M is hyperkähler (e.g., M = ℝ4, K3, or a torus). The deformation complex is Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) → Ω2,+(ad P), where the last term is the self‑dual part of Ω2. Its index is given by the Atiyah‑Singer theorem: for G = SU(2) and M simply connected, vdim = 8k − 3(1 − b1 + b2,+). The ADHM construction (Atiyah‑Drinfeld‑Hitchin‑Manin, 1978) provides an explicit description of all instantons on ℝ4 as a hyperkähler quotient of a finite‑dimensional vector space. The universal curvature ℱ over M × Mk generates differential forms on the moduli space; the natural symplectic forms are ωI = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δ𝒜), ωJ = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ * δ𝒜), ωK = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ * δ𝒜) with appropriate complex structures. Donaldson’s polynomial invariants (1983) are defined by integrating cohomology classes on Mk against the fundamental class of the compactified moduli space (Uhlenbeck compactification). The compactification adds ideal instantons (point‑like singularities) and the boundary strata are products of lower‑charge moduli spaces with symmetric products of M. Examiners frequently test the ADHM data (matrices B1, B2, I, J satisfying the ADHM equations), the dimension formula, and the fact that the moduli space is non‑compact but admits a natural compactification. A common mistake is to confuse the instanton number k with the second Chern class c2(E); for SU(2) bundles k = c2(E). Another trap is to assume the moduli space is always smooth; singularities occur at reducible connections, which are excluded by considering only irreducible solutions or by working with the framed moduli space.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Hitchin Equations on Higher Dimensional Varieties

The Hitchin equations on a compact Kähler manifold X of complex dimension n generalize the two‑dimensional Higgs bundle equations: FA + [Φ, Φ*] = λ·Id·ωn, ∂̄AΦ = 0, where A is a unitary connection on a holomorphic vector bundle E, Φ ∈ Ω1,0(End E) is the Higgs field, and λ = 2πμ(E)/Vol(X). For n = 1 this reduces to the classical Hitchin system on a Riemann surface. Simpson (1988, 1992) proved that solutions correspond to polystable Higgs bundles with vanishing Chern classes. The moduli space MHit(X,E) of solutions modulo gauge transformations carries a natural hyperkähler structure when X is a curve; for higher dimensions it is a Kähler manifold with a holomorphic symplectic form Ω = ∫X Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δΦ) ∧ ωn−1. The deformation complex is Ω0(ad E) → Ω0,1(ad E) ⊕ Ω1,0(ad E) → Ω1,1(ad E) ⊕ Ω2,0(ad E), and its virtual dimension is given by the index of this complex, which can be expressed in terms of Chern characters of E and the Todd class of X. The Hitchin fibration h: MHit → ⊕i=1r H0(X, KXi) (where r = rank E) is an algebraically completely integrable system; the generic fibre is an abelian variety (a Prym variety of the spectral cover). The differential forms on the total space are generated by the universal Higgs field and the universal connection; the holomorphic symplectic form is exact in the sense of the hyperkähler quotient construction. Examiners often ask for the stability condition for Higgs bundles on higher‑dimensional varieties (slope stability with respect to the Kähler class), the expression of the virtual dimension via the Riemann‑Roch theorem, and the fact that the Hitchin fibration is proper with Lagrangian fibres. A frequent error is to assume the moduli space is hyperkähler for any n > 1; the hyperkähler structure exists only when X is a curve or when one considers the moduli space of solutions on a product X = Σ × Y with a suitable twist.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin‑Witten Equations on Manifolds with Boundary

The Kapustin‑Witten (KW) equations on a four‑manifold M with boundary ∂M are a one‑parameter family of first‑order equations for a connection A and a one‑form Φ ∈ Ω1(ad P): (* − t)FA = dAΦ, (* + t−1)dAΦ = 0, where t ∈ ℂℙ1 is the complexified coupling. When M has boundary, one must impose boundary conditions to obtain a well‑posed elliptic problem. The two most studied are the Nahm‑pole boundary condition (Φ ∼ t−1·ρ/y + … near ∂M, where ρ is a principal 𝔰𝔲(2) embedding) and the Dirichlet‑type condition fixing the restriction of A and the tangential component of Φ to a prescribed solution of the Hitchin equations on ∂M. The moduli space MKW(M,∂M;t) of solutions modulo gauge transformations that are identity on the boundary carries a natural symplectic structure for generic t. The deformation complex is Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) ⊕ Ω1(ad P) → Ω2(ad P) ⊕ Ω2(ad P) with boundary conditions given by the linearisation of the chosen boundary condition. Its index computes the virtual dimension; for the Nahm‑pole condition on M = Σ × [0,∞) the dimension equals the dimension of the Hitchin moduli space on Σ. The universal fields (𝒜, Φ) over M × MKW generate differential forms; the symplectic form is ω = ∫M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δΦ) + boundary term ∫∂M Tr(δ𝒜 ∧ δΦ). The work of Gaiotto‑Witten (2008) and Kapustin‑Witten (2007) relates the category of boundary conditions to the geometric Langlands correspondence: the Nahm‑pole condition corresponds to the “canonical coisotropic brane” and the Dirichlet condition to the “B‑brane” of the Hitchin system. Examiners may ask for the linearised boundary conditions, the identification of the parameter t with the complex structure on the Hitchin base, and the fact that the KW equations at t = ±1 reduce to the (anti‑)self‑dual Yang‑Mills equations. A common trap is to treat the boundary term in the symplectic form as negligible; it is essential for the symplectic structure to be non‑degenerate and for the gluing formula for the partition function.

  • Confusing the Hermitian Yang‑Mills equations with the ordinary Yang‑Mills equations; the former are first‑order and require a Kähler background.
  • Assuming the Bogomolny moduli space on a compact three‑manifold is hyperkähler without imposing asymptotic or boundary conditions; the hyperkähler metric exists only for ℝ3 or with Nahm‑pole boundaries.
  • Mixing the instanton number k with the second Chern class for groups other than SU(2); for general G the instanton number is defined via an invariant polynomial on the Lie algebra.
  • Believing the Hitchin moduli space on a higher‑dimensional variety is hyperkähler; it is only Kähler with a holomorphic symplectic form, and hyperkähler only for curves.
  • Omitting the boundary contribution in the symplectic form for Kapustin‑Witten equations on manifolds with boundary; the boundary term is required for non‑degeneracy and for the correct gluing law.
  • Treating the virtual dimension as the actual dimension of the moduli space; obstructions can make the actual dimension larger, and the virtual dimension is the Euler characteristic of the obstruction theory.
  • Misidentifying the parameter t in the Kapustin‑Witten equations as a real coupling constant; it is a point on ℂℙ1 and ellipticity holds only for t ∈ ℝ ∪ iℝ.

These advanced topics illustrate how differential forms encode the geometry of moduli spaces arising from gauge‑theoretic PDEs and provide the computational tools examiners expect candidates to master.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Haydys–Witten Equations on Five‑Manifolds

The Haydys–Witten equations are a first‑order system on a five‑dimensional Riemannian manifold M for a connection A on a principal G‑bundle and a two‑form B with values in the adjoint bundle. They read FA = *dAB and dA*B = 0, where * is the Hodge star of the metric on M. These equations arise from the dimensional reduction of the seven‑dimensional G₂‑instanton equations and play a central role in the mathematical formulation of M‑theory. The moduli space MHW(M) of solutions modulo gauge transformations carries a natural symplectic structure induced by the pairing ω(δ₁,δ₂) = ∫M Tr(δ₁A ∧ δ₂B − δ₂A ∧ δ₁B). The deformation complex is Ω⁰(ad P) → Ω¹(ad P) ⊕ Ω²(ad P) → Ω³(ad P) ⊕ Ω⁴(ad P) with differentials given by the linearisation of the equations. Its index, computed by the Atiyah–Singer theorem, gives the virtual dimension vdim = ∫M (c₂(ad P) ∧ p₁(TM) + …). When M has a boundary, one must impose elliptic boundary conditions such as the Nahm‑pole type for B and a Dirichlet condition for the tangential part of A. The universal fields (𝒜, ℬ) over M × MHW generate differential forms on the moduli space; the symplectic form is exact in the sense of the hyperkähler quotient construction when M is a product Σ × ℝ². Examiners often ask for the explicit form of the linearised boundary conditions, the identification of the virtual dimension with the index of the deformation complex, and the fact that the Haydys–Witten equations reduce to the Bogomolny equations on ℝ³ after a further reduction. A common error is to treat the symplectic form as degenerate without the boundary contribution; the boundary term is essential for non‑degeneracy and for the gluing formula in the associated topological field theory.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Generalized Hitchin Equations on Calabi–Yau Four‑Folds

The generalized Hitchin equations on a Calabi–Yau four‑fold X involve a unitary connection A on a holomorphic vector bundle E and a (2,0)‑form Φ ∈ Ω²,⁰(End E) satisfying FA⁰,² = 0, ∂̄AΦ = 0, and the moment‑map condition ω² ∧ (FA + [Φ, Φ*]) = c·ω⁴ where ω is the Kähler form and c a topological constant. These equations are the dimensional reduction of the Hermitian Yang–Mills equations on a Calabi–Yau five‑fold and define a moduli space MGH(X) that carries a holomorphic symplectic form Ω = ∫X Tr(δA ∧ δΦ) ∧ ω². The deformation complex is Ω⁰(End E) → Ω¹(End E) ⊕ Ω²,⁰(End E) → Ω²(End E) ⊕ Ω³,⁰(End E) with differentials given by the linearised equations. Its index, evaluated via the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem, yields the virtual dimension vdim = ∫X ch(End E) ∧ td(X). When X is a product S × Σ of a K3 surface and a Riemann surface, the moduli space is hyperkähler and can be identified with the moduli space of Higgs bundles on Σ twisted by the K3. The universal fields (𝒜, Φ) generate the tangent‑obstruction theory; the holomorphic symplectic form is closed and non‑degenerate on the smooth locus. Examiners may ask for the expression of the virtual dimension in terms of Chern characters, the role of the stability condition (slope stability with respect to ω), and the fact that the moduli space is proper when X is compact. A frequent trap is to assume the moduli space is hyperkähler for any Calabi–Yau four‑fold; the hyperkähler structure exists only in the product case or after a suitable twist.

Technique: Virtual dimension via index theorem
Compute the virtual dimension of the moduli space of solutions to the generalized Hitchin equations on a compact Calabi–Yau four‑fold X with gauge group SU(2) and c₂(E) = 5.
The deformation complex is 0 → Ω⁰(ad E) → Ω¹(ad E) ⊕ Ω²,⁰(ad E) → Ω²(ad E) ⊕ Ω³,⁰(ad E) → 0. Its index equals ∫X ch(ad E) ∧ td(X). For SU(2), ad E ≅ End₀ E has rank 3 and Chern character ch(ad E) = 3 − c₂(E) + …. On a Calabi–Yau four‑fold, td(X) = 1 + ½c₂(X) + …. The degree‑8 component of the product is −c₂(E) ∧ ½c₂(X). Integrating gives vdim = −½ ∫X c₂(E) ∧ c₂(X). With c₂(E)=5 and assuming ∫X c₂(X) = 24 (as for a K3×K3), vdim = −½·5·24 = −60. The negative sign indicates an obstruction; the actual dimension is the Euler characteristic of the obstruction sheaf, which examiners may ask to compute via Serre duality.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the virtual dimension of a moduli space defined by first‑order PDEs, write down the deformation complex, identify its symbol sequence, and apply the Atiyah–Singer index theorem. Keep the Chern character and Todd class formulas for the relevant bundles at hand; the degree‑top component is the only one that contributes. If the manifold is a product, use the Künneth decomposition to simplify the integral.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations with Defect Operators

The Kapustin–Witten equations on a four‑manifold M with a codimension‑two defect D are a one‑parameter family t ∈ ℂℙ¹ for a connection A and a one‑form Φ ∈ Ω¹(ad P) satisfying (*−t)FA = dAΦ and (*+t⁻¹)dAΦ = 0 away from D. Along D one prescribes a singularity modelled on a representation of the affine Lie algebra ĝ at level k; the defect operator creates a prescribed monodromy for A and a pole of order one for Φ with residue in a chosen nilpotent orbit. The moduli space MKW(M,D;t) carries a symplectic form ω = ∫MD Tr(δA ∧ δΦ) + ∫D Tr(δA∥ ∧ δΦ∥) where the boundary term on D involves the tangential components. The deformation complex is the same as for the smooth Kapustin–Witten equations but with boundary conditions at D given by the linearisation of the defect singularity. Its index computes the virtual dimension, which equals the dimension of the corresponding moduli space of parabolic Higgs bundles on a Riemann surface when M = Σ × ℝ². The universal fields generate differential forms on the moduli space; the symplectic form is exact in the sense of the Hamiltonian reduction by the gauge group preserving the defect. Gaiotto and Witten showed that the category of defect operators is equivalent to the category of modules over the quantum group Uq(ĝ) with q = eπi t. Examiners may ask for the explicit form of the defect boundary conditions, the identification of the parameter t with the complex structure on the Hitchin base, and the fact that at t = ±1 the equations reduce to the (anti‑)self‑dual Yang–Mills equations with a prescribed singularity. A common mistake is to omit the defect contribution to the symplectic form; without it the form is degenerate and the gluing law for partition functions fails.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Segal Program for Counting Special Lagrangians

The Donaldson–Segal program proposes a Kuranishi‑type virtual fundamental cycle on the moduli space MSL(X) of special Lagrangian submanifolds in a Calabi–Yau n‑fold X. The deformation theory is governed by the elliptic complex Ω⁰(L) → Ω¹(L) → Ωⁿ⁻¹(L) where L is a special Lagrangian and the maps are given by the linearised special Lagrangian equations dα = 0, d*α = 0 together with the phase condition. The virtual dimension is zero, and the virtual fundamental class is constructed by integrating the Euler class of the obstruction bundle over the Kuranishi charts. Differential forms on the ambient manifold X pull back to forms on the universal family 𝒰 → MSL; the integration over the fibre yields cohomology classes on the moduli space that are used to define open Gromov–Witten invariants. The program relates these invariants to the Fukaya category via the homological mirror symmetry conjecture. Examiners often ask for the expression of the virtual dimension as the index of the deformation complex, the role of the Maslov index in grading the Kuranishi structure, and the fact that the virtual fundamental cycle is independent of the choices of perturbations. A frequent trap is to confuse the virtual dimension (zero) with the actual dimension of the moduli space, which can be positive due to obstructions; the virtual cycle captures the enumerative information correctly only after the obstruction bundle is taken into account.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Haydys–Witten Equations on Manifolds with G₂‑Holonomy Cones

When the five‑manifold M is the link of a G₂‑holonomy cone C(M), the Haydys–Witten equations acquire an additional scaling symmetry. The moduli space MHW(M) then carries a conical hyperkähler metric, and the differential forms generated by the universal fields are homogeneous with respect to the Euler vector field. The deformation complex is equivariant under the ℝ⁺‑action, and its index can be computed by the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer theorem on the cone with appropriate boundary conditions at the tip. The virtual dimension equals the dimension of the moduli space of G₂‑instantons on the cone, which is given by a topological formula involving the Pontryagin classes of the bundle and the G₂‑structure. The universal curvature and the universal two‑form ℬ satisfy a transversality condition that ensures the symplectic form is non‑degenerate on the smooth locus. Examiners may ask for the relationship between the Haydys–Witten equations on the link and the G₂‑instanton equations on the cone, the role of the η‑invariant in the index formula, and the fact that the moduli space is a hyperkähler cone only when the link is a Sasaki–Einstein manifold. A common error is to assume the hyperkähler metric exists for any G₂‑cone; the Sasaki–Einstein condition is necessary for the triholomorphic Euler vector field.

  • Assuming the virtual dimension equals the actual dimension of the moduli space; obstructions can make the actual dimension larger.
  • Omitting the boundary or defect contribution in the symplectic form; the extra term is essential for non‑degeneracy and gluing.
  • Confusing the parameter t in the Kapustin–Witten equations with a real coupling constant; it is a point on ℂℙ¹ and ellipticity holds only for t ∈ ℝ ∪ iℝ.
  • Treating the Haydys–Witten equations as elliptic without imposing suitable boundary conditions at infinity or at a defect.
  • Believing the generalized Hitchin moduli space on a Calabi–Yau four‑fold is hyperkähler for any X; it is only Kähler with a holomorphic symplectic form, hyperkähler only in product cases.
  • Neglecting the Maslov index when grading the Kuranishi structure in the Donaldson–Segal program; the grading is crucial for the virtual fundamental cycle.
  • Misidentifying the instanton number for groups other than SU(2); for general G the instanton number is defined via an invariant polynomial on the Lie algebra.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations on Non‑Compact Four‑Manifolds

The Vafa–Witten equations arise from the topological twist of N = 4 super Yang–Mills theory on a four‑manifold X. When X is non‑compact, the moduli space MVW(X) of solutions to the equations F+ = [φ, φ*] and dAφ = 0 (with φ a complex adjoint scalar) requires a careful treatment of the asymptotic behaviour of fields. Standard practice is to impose weighted Sobolev space conditions at infinity so that the linearised deformation complex becomes Fredholm. The virtual dimension of MVW is given by the index of the elliptic complex Ω0(ad P) → Ω1(ad P) ⊕ Ω0(ad P) → Ω2,+(ad P) ⊕ Ω1(ad P), which evaluates to 4 c2(P) − 3(1 − b1 + b2,+) for a principal G‑bundle P. On asymptotically locally Euclidean (ALE) or asymptotically locally flat (ALF) spaces, the index receives contributions from the boundary at infinity that are captured by the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer η‑invariant of the induced operator on the link. The resulting moduli space carries a natural hyperkähler metric constructed from the L2 inner product on the space of harmonic forms representing tangent vectors. A key result is that the hyperkähler quotient construction of the moduli space coincides with the space of solutions to the Vafa–Witten equations modulo gauge, provided the stability condition (no proper invariant sub‑bundle with non‑negative degree) holds. Examiners often ask for the explicit form of the deformation complex, the role of the η‑invariant in the index formula, and the fact that the hyperkähler metric is complete only when the asymptotic cone is a hyperkähler quotient of a flat space. A common trap is to ignore the boundary contribution to the symplectic form; without it the form is degenerate and the gluing law for partition functions fails.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Corners

The Kapustin–Witten equations are a one‑parameter family of gauge‑theoretic equations on a four‑manifold M that interpolate between the (anti‑)self‑dual Yang–Mills equations and the Hitchin equations. When M has corners (i.e. a manifold with boundary and codimension‑two strata), the moduli space MKW(M, t) of solutions depends critically on the choice of boundary conditions at each stratum. The natural boundary conditions are of Nahm‑pole type on the boundary components and of Dirichlet/Neumann type on the corner strata, ensuring ellipticity of the linearised operator in weighted Hölder spaces. The deformation complex is a double complex that combines the bulk elliptic complex with the boundary and corner complexes; its index is computed by a version of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with corners, involving η‑invariants on the links of the corners. The virtual dimension equals the sum of the bulk index and the contributions from the boundary and corner data, which can be expressed in terms of characteristic classes of the bundle and the geometry of the corner strata. The moduli space carries a natural symplectic form obtained by integrating the universal curvature over the fundamental class of M together with boundary terms that render the form non‑degenerate. In the context of the geometric Langlands program, the parameter t ∈ ℂℙ1 corresponds to the complex structure on the Hitchin base, and the corner data encode the ramification data for the Langlands dual group. Examiners frequently test the identification of the correct boundary conditions for a given value of t, the computation of the index via the corner‑corrected APS formula, and the verification that the symplectic form is closed and non‑degenerate only after including the corner contributions. A frequent error is to treat the Kapustin–Witten equations as elliptic on a manifold with corners without imposing the Nahm‑pole condition, which leads to a non‑Fredholm problem.

Worked Example: Index Computation on a Manifold with Corners
Let M be a compact oriented four‑manifold with boundary ∂M = Y ∪ Z meeting at a corner C = Y ∩ Z. Consider the Kapustin–Witten equations at t = 1 (self‑dual Yang–Mills) with Nahm‑pole boundary condition on Y and Dirichlet condition on Z. Compute the virtual dimension of the moduli space for an SU(2) bundle with instanton number k.
The bulk index for the self‑dual deformation complex is 8k − 3(1 − b1(M) + b2,+(M)). The Nahm‑pole condition on Y contributes −dim G = −3 to the index (the dimension of the space of residues). The Dirichlet condition on Z contributes 0. The corner C contributes an η‑invariant term η(C) which for a product corner equals half the signature of the link. Summing, the virtual dimension = 8k − 3(1 − b1 + b2,+) − 3 + η(C). For a simply‑connected M with b2,+ = 1 and trivial corner link, η = 0, giving 8k − 6.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for the virtual dimension of a moduli space on a manifold with boundary or corners, first write down the bulk elliptic complex and its index formula. Then list each stratum (boundary components, corners) and the associated boundary condition; recall the standard index contributions: Nahm‑pole gives −dim G, Dirichlet gives 0, Neumann gives +dim G. Finally, add the η‑invariant of the corner link if the corner is non‑product. This systematic approach avoids missing boundary terms that examiners frequently test.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Haydys–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Boundary

The Haydys–Witten equations are a system of first‑order gauge‑theoretic equations on a five‑manifold M that arise from the dimensional reduction of the self‑duality equations in seven dimensions. When M has a non‑empty boundary ∂M, the moduli space MHW(M) of solutions modulo gauge transformations that preserve the boundary data is a finite‑dimensional space carrying a natural symplectic structure. The appropriate boundary conditions are of two types: (i) a Dirichlet condition fixing the pull‑back of the connection and the scalar field to a prescribed boundary configuration, and (ii) a Neumann‑type condition that sets the normal component of the curvature and the normal derivative of the scalar to zero. The linearised deformation complex is an elliptic complex of differential forms with values in the adjoint bundle, and its index is computed by the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer theorem for manifolds with boundary, yielding a formula that involves the η‑invariant of the induced operator on ∂M. The virtual dimension equals the bulk index plus the boundary contribution, which for a product boundary ∂M = Y × [0,1] reduces to the spectral flow of the family of boundary operators. The universal curvature and the universal two‑form B satisfy a transversality condition that guarantees the symplectic form ω = ∫M Tr(δA ∧ δA) + ∫∂M Tr(δφ ∧ δA) is non‑degenerate on the smooth locus. In the special case where M is the link of a G₂‑holonomy cone, the Haydys–Witten equations acquire an additional scaling symmetry and the moduli space becomes a hyperkähler cone; the Sasaki–Einstein condition on the link is necessary and sufficient for the existence of a triholomorphic Euler vector field. Examiners often ask for the explicit form of the boundary conditions that yield a well‑posed elliptic problem, the role of the η‑invariant in the index formula, and the relationship between the Haydys–Witten moduli space and the moduli space of G₂‑instantons on the cone. A common mistake is to assume the hyperkähler metric exists for any G₂‑cone; the Sasaki–Einstein condition is essential.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Generalized Hitchin Equations on Non‑Kähler Manifolds

The generalized Hitchin equations extend the classical Hitchin system to a pair consisting of a connection A on a principal G‑bundle and a Higgs field φ taking values in the adjoint bundle twisted by a closed three‑form H. On a non‑Kähler complex manifold X with a Hermitian metric ω and a non‑zero torsion three‑form H = dcω, the equations read FA0,2 = 0, FA1,1 ∧ ωn−1 + [φ, φ*] ∧ ωn−1 = 0, and dAφ = 0, where the adjoint is taken with respect to the metric and the H‑twisted differential. The moduli space MGH(X) of solutions modulo gauge carries a natural Kähler metric induced from the L2 inner product on the space of harmonic representatives of the deformation complex. The deformation complex is the H‑twisted Dolbeault complex Ω0,•(ad P) with differential ∂A + φ∧, and its index is given by the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem twisted by the bundle ad P and the line bundle determined by H. When X is a Calabi–Yau threefold with a non‑Kähler metric satisfying the Strominger system, the generalized Hitchin equations are equivalent to the supersymmetry conditions of heterotic string theory. The virtual dimension equals the Euler characteristic of the twisted complex, which can be expressed in terms of Chern characters and the Todd class of X. A key feature is that the moduli space is not hyperkähler in general; it is Kähler with a holomorphic symplectic form, and becomes hyperkähler only when X is a product of a K3 surface and a torus. Examiners may ask for the derivation of the deformation complex, the computation of the virtual dimension via the index theorem, and the distinction between the Kähler and hyperkähler cases. A frequent trap is to confuse the generalized Hitchin moduli space with the ordinary Hitchin moduli space on a Kähler manifold; the presence of the torsion three‑form H modifies both the equations and the symplectic structure.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Non‑Compact Calabi–Yau Threefolds

Donaldson–Thomas (DT) theory counts ideal sheaves (or equivalently, stable pairs) on a Calabi–Yau threefold X via a virtual fundamental class on the moduli space MDT(X). When X is non‑compact (e.g. the total space of the canonical bundle over a del Pezzo surface), the moduli space is non‑compact and the standard construction of the virtual class using a perfect obstruction theory must be supplemented with a properness condition on the support of the sheaves. The deformation theory is governed by the Ext complex Ext•(I, I)0 for an ideal sheaf I, and the virtual dimension is zero. The virtual fundamental class is defined by integrating the Euler class of the obstruction bundle over the Kuranishi charts, which are locally modelled on the critical locus of a holomorphic function (the superpotential). On non‑compact X, one often works with a torus action that has compact fixed loci; the virtual localisation formula then expresses DT invariants as a sum over fixed points of a rational function in the equivariant parameters. The generating series of DT invariants satisfies the wall‑crossing formula of Kontsevich–Soibelman, which relates invariants in different chambers of the stability manifold. The differential forms that appear in the localisation computation are the equivariant Chern characters of the universal sheaf on X × MDT, pushed forward to MDT via the projection. These forms satisfy the holomorphic anomaly equation, which governs their dependence on the Kähler moduli. Examiners frequently test the set‑up of the perfect obstruction theory, the application of the virtual localisation formula to a concrete toric Calabi–Yau threefold (e.g. the resolved conifold), and the statement of the wall‑crossing formula for a simple quiver. A common error is to assume the virtual class is a genuine fundamental class; it is only a cycle in the Chow group of the moduli space, and its degree depends on the choice of stability condition.

  • Confusing the bulk index formula with the full index on manifolds with boundary or corners; always add boundary η‑invariants and corner contributions.
  • Omitting the Nahm‑pole or Dirichlet boundary terms in the symplectic form for Kapustin–Witten or Haydys–Witten moduli spaces; the form becomes degenerate without them.
  • Assuming the generalized Hitchin moduli space on a non‑Kähler manifold is hyperkähler; it is only Kähler with a holomorphic symplectic form unless the manifold is a product of K3 and a torus.
  • Treating the Donaldson–Thomas virtual class on a non‑compact Calabi–Yau threefold as an ordinary fundamental class; it is a virtual cycle defined via a perfect obstruction theory and depends on the stability chamber.
  • Misidentifying the instanton number for groups other than SU(2); for a general group G the instanton number is defined via an invariant polynomial on the Lie algebra, not simply the second Chern class.
  • Neglecting the Maslov index when grading the Kuranishi structure in the Donaldson–Segal program; the grading is crucial for the virtual fundamental cycle to be well defined.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Hitchin Equations on Riemann Surfaces

The Hitchin equations on a compact Riemann surface Σ of genus g ≥ 2 are a system of first‑order partial differential equations for a pair (A, φ) consisting of a unitary connection A on a rank‑n Hermitian vector bundle E → Σ and a Higgs field φ ∈ Ω¹,⁰(Σ, End₀(E)). The equations read F_A + [φ, φ*] = 0 and ∂̄_A φ = 0, where F_A is the curvature of A and φ* is the adjoint with respect to the Hermitian metric. Solutions correspond to stable Higgs bundles via the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence. The moduli space M_H(Σ, n, d) of solutions with fixed topological type (rank n, degree d) carries a natural hyperkähler structure; its complex structures I, J, K arise from the three symplectic forms ω_I = ∫_Σ Tr(δA ∧ δA + δφ ∧ δφ*), ω_J = ∫_Σ Tr(δA ∧ δφ + δφ ∧ δA), ω_K = ∫_Σ Tr(δA ∧ δφ* − δφ* ∧ δA). The de Rham complex of differential forms on M_H is filtered by the weight decomposition under the natural ℂ*‑action (t·(A, φ) = (A, tφ)). The associated graded pieces are isomorphic to the cohomology of the Hitchin fibration h: M_H → ⊕i=1^n H⁰(Σ, K^i), where K is the canonical bundle. The generic fibre is an abelian variety (a Prym variety of the spectral curve). The topology of M_H is encoded in the perverse Leray filtration, which coincides with the weight filtration on the cohomology of the corresponding character variety via the non‑abelian Hodge isomorphism. Examiners often ask for the dimension formula dim_ℂ M_H = 2(n²(g−1)+1) and for the description of the Hitchin section (the “SL₂‑embedding”) which gives a holomorphic Lagrangian section of h. A common trap is to confuse the hyperkähler metric on M_H with the natural Kähler metric on the moduli space of stable bundles; the former is complete and has polynomial volume growth, the latter is incomplete.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Corners

The Kapustin–Witten equations are a one‑parameter family of gauge‑theoretic equations on a four‑manifold M with boundary ∂M, interpolating between the anti‑self‑dual Yang–Mills equations (t = 0) and the Hitchin equations on a Riemann surface (t = ∞). When M is a manifold with corners (e.g. a product Σ × C where Σ is a Riemann surface with boundary and C is a surface with corners), the equations acquire additional boundary and corner conditions. The fields are a connection A on a principal G‑bundle and a Lie‑algebra‑valued one‑form φ. The equations read (F_A − φ ∧ φ)⁺ = 0, d_A φ = 0, d_A * φ = 0, together with the Nahm‑pole boundary condition on each boundary hypersurface: near a boundary component, φ ∼ t⁻¹ α + regular, where α is a fixed regular element of the Lie algebra. At corners where two boundary hypersurfaces meet, the residues α₁, α₂ must commute, defining a Levi subgroup L ⊂ G. The moduli space M_KW(M, G, t) of solutions modulo gauge transformations preserving the boundary data carries a natural symplectic form ω = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δφ) + ∑boundary ∫∂M Tr(δA ∧ δφ) + ∑corners ∫corner Tr(δA ∧ δφ). The corner contributions are essential: without them the form is degenerate. The virtual dimension is given by the index of the deformation complex, which is a twisted version of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with corners. The index formula includes bulk terms (Chern–Weil integrals), boundary η‑invariants, and corner contributions expressed via the relative Chern–Simons invariants of the commuting residues. Examiners may ask to derive the corner term in the symplectic form or to compute the virtual dimension for G = SU(2) on a product of a pair of pants with an interval. A frequent error is to omit the corner term and claim the symplectic form is non‑degenerate on the bulk‑plus‑boundary space.

Worked Example: Corner Contribution to the Symplectic Form
Let M = Σ × I where Σ is a Riemann surface with two boundary circles and I = [0,1] is an interval. The corners are four points. For G = SU(2), the Nahm‑pole residues at the two boundary components of Σ are α₁ = diag(i,−i) and α₂ = diag(−i,i). Compute the corner contribution to the symplectic form ω_corner = ∑p∈corners Tr(δA_p ∧ δφ_p).
At each corner the residue of φ is the sum of the two commuting residues α₁ + α₂ = 0. The variation δφ_p is constrained to lie in the centraliser of the residue, which is the maximal torus T ⊂ SU(2). The connection variation δA_p takes values in the Lie algebra of the stabiliser, also T. The trace pairing on T is non‑degenerate. Since there are four corners, ω_corner = 4 ∫_T Tr(δa ∧ δφ) where a, φ are the torus components. This term cancels the degeneracy coming from the bulk‑plus‑boundary integral, rendering ω non‑degenerate on the full moduli space.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem mentions manifolds with corners, immediately write down the three pieces of the symplectic form: bulk, boundary, corner. The corner term is often the only place where the commutator of residues appears; check that the residues commute and identify the Levi subgroup. For index computations, recall the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer formula with corner corrections: index = ∫_M Â(R) ch(E) − ½(η(∂M) + h) + corner term. Memorise the corner term for a product of two manifolds with boundary: it is the relative Chern–Simons invariant of the commuting boundary connections.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations on Four‑Manifolds with Boundary

The Vafa–Witten equations are a topological twist of N = 4 super‑Yang–Mills theory on a four‑manifold X. They involve a connection A on a principal G‑bundle and a self‑dual two‑form B ∈ Ω²₊(X, ad P) together with a scalar field φ ∈ Ω⁰(X, ad P). The equations are F_A⁺ = [B, B] + d_A φ, d_A B = 0, d_A * B = 0, and d_A * d_A φ = 0. On a closed manifold the moduli space M_VW(X, G) is expected to be a compact, oriented, finite‑dimensional space whose virtual fundamental class yields invariants related to S‑duality. When X has a boundary ∂X = Y, one must impose boundary conditions preserving half of the supersymmetry. The natural choice is the “Dirichlet” condition for the connection (A|_Y = a fixed flat connection a) and a Nahm‑pole type condition for B and φ: near the boundary, B ∼ t⁻¹ β + regular, φ ∼ t⁻¹ γ + regular, where β, γ are elements of the Lie algebra satisfying [β, γ] = 0. The boundary data define a Lagrangian submanifold L_Y ⊂ M_VW(Y, G) of the moduli space on the three‑manifold Y (which is the space of solutions to the Bogomolny equations). The moduli space with boundary M_VW(X, Y; a, β, γ) carries a symplectic form ω = ∫_X Tr(δA ∧ δB + δφ ∧ δ*B) + ∫_Y Tr(δa ∧ δβ + δγ ∧ δ*β). The boundary term is precisely the pullback of the canonical symplectic form on M_VW(Y, G). The virtual dimension is computed by the index of the deformation complex, which is a twisted version of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with boundary. The index formula includes the bulk Chern–Weil term ∫_X (c₂(ad P) − ¼ p₁(ad P)), the boundary η‑invariant of the odd‑signature operator on Y twisted by ad P, and a contribution from the Nahm‑pole residues. Examiners often test the identification of the boundary moduli space with the moduli of solutions to the Bogomolny equations on Y and the computation of the virtual dimension for G = SU(2) on a four‑manifold with boundary a homology three‑sphere. A common mistake is to treat the boundary term as a mere correction rather than a symplectic reduction that identifies the bulk moduli space with the fibre product of the bulk and boundary moduli spaces over the boundary symplectic manifold.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Compact Calabi–Yau Threefolds
