Donaldson–Thomas (DT) theory on a compact Calabi–Yau threefold X counts ideal sheaves I_Z ⊂ O_X (or stable pairs (F, s)) with a virtual fundamental class [M_DT(X)]^vir ∈ A0(M_DT(X)). The moduli space M_DT(X) is proper because X is compact, unlike the non‑compact case where one must impose support conditions. The deformation theory is governed by the Ext complex Ext^•(I, I)0, whose cohomology groups are Ext¹ (tangent space) and Ext² (obstruction space). The virtual dimension is zero, as expected for a Calabi–Yau threefold. The perfect obstruction theory is constructed from the Atiyah class of the universal ideal sheaf on X × M_DT(X). The virtual fundamental class is obtained by intersecting the intrinsic normal cone with the zero section of the obstruction bundle. The Behrend function ν: M_DT(X) → ℤ gives a constructible function whose weighted Euler characteristic equals the DT invariant: DT(X) = ∑n∈ℤ n χ(ν⁻¹(n)). On a compact X, the generating series of DT invariants satisfies the wall‑crossing formula of Kontsevich–Soibelman, which relates invariants in different chambers of the stability manifold. The differential forms that appear in the localisation computation (when a torus action with compact fixed loci exists) are the equivariant Chern characters of the universal sheaf pushed forward to M_DT(X). These forms satisfy the holomorphic anomaly equation, which governs their dependence on the Kähler moduli. Examiners frequently ask for the construction of the perfect obstruction theory, the statement of the wall‑crossing formula for the conifold, and the proof that the virtual dimension is zero using the Calabi–Yau condition (trivial canonical bundle). A common error is to assume the virtual class is a genuine fundamental class; it is only a cycle in the Chow group, and its degree depends on the stability condition.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Seiberg–Witten Equations on Three‑Manifolds

The three‑dimensional Seiberg–Witten equations on a closed oriented Riemannian three‑manifold Y involve a spin^c structure 𝔰, a connection A on the determinant line bundle L = det(𝔰), and a spinor ψ ∈ Γ(S) where S is the spinor bundle. The equations are F_A = ψ ψ* − ½|ψ|² g and D_A ψ = 0, where D_A is the Dirac operator. The moduli space M_SW(Y, 𝔰) of solutions modulo gauge transformations is a compact, smooth manifold of dimension d = (c₁(L)² − 2χ(Y) − 3σ(Y))/4, where χ is the Euler characteristic and σ the signature (zero for three‑manifolds). The expected dimension simplifies to d = ½ ∫_Y c₁(L) ∧ c₁(L) − ½ χ(Y). The moduli space carries a natural orientation determined by a homology orientation of Y. The Seiberg–Witten invariant SW(Y, 𝔰) is the signed count of points when d = 0, or the evaluation of the cohomology class μ(Σ) on the fundamental class when d > 0, where μ(Σ) is the cohomology class associated to a surface Σ ⊂ Y. The differential forms representing μ(Σ) are constructed from the curvature of the universal connection over the configuration space modulo gauge. The wall‑crossing phenomenon for three‑manifolds with b₁ > 0 is governed by the chamber structure of the space of metrics and perturbations. Examiners often test the dimension formula, the construction of the μ‑map, and the wall‑crossing formula for a three‑manifold with b₁ = 1. A frequent trap is to confuse the three‑dimensional Seiberg–Witten equations with the four‑dimensional monopole equations; the former have a scalar curvature term in the Weitzenböck formula that changes the sign of the curvature contribution.

  • Confusing the hyperkähler structure on the Hitchin moduli space with the Kähler structure on the moduli of stable bundles; the former is complete, the latter is not.
  • Omitting the corner term in the Kapustin–Witten symplectic form, leading to a degenerate form on the moduli space with corners.
  • Treating the boundary term in the Vafa–Witten symplectic form as a mere correction instead of a symplectic reduction identifying bulk and boundary moduli.
  • Assuming the Donaldson–Thomas virtual class on a compact Calabi–Yau threefold is an ordinary fundamental class; it is a virtual cycle depending on the stability chamber.
  • Mixing the three‑dimensional Seiberg–Witten dimension formula with the four‑dimensional monopole formula; the curvature term has opposite sign in the Weitzenböck identity.
  • Neglecting the Nahm‑pole residue commutativity condition at corners, which defines the Levi subgroup and is essential for the deformation complex.
  • Forgetting that the Behrend function is constructible and its weighted Euler characteristic computes DT invariants, not the ordinary Euler characteristic of the moduli space.

The advanced topics above illustrate how differential forms encode symplectic structures, index theorems, and virtual fundamental classes across a spectrum of gauge‑theoretic moduli problems.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Corners

The Kapustin–Witten equations arise from the topological twisting of N=4 super Yang–Mills theory in four dimensions. When the underlying four‑manifold M has corners of codimension two, the moduli space of solutions acquires additional boundary strata corresponding to Nahm‑pole type singularities along the corner loci. The differential forms that represent the symplectic structure on this moduli space are constructed from the bulk Chern–Simons functional together with corner contributions given by integrals of the form ∫_C Tr(φ ∧ d_A φ) where C denotes a corner component and φ is the Higgs field. The corner term is not a mere correction; it ensures that the total symplectic form is non‑degenerate on the extended configuration space and that the Hamiltonian reduction by the gauge group yields a genuine symplectic quotient. The deformation complex at a solution splits into a bulk elliptic complex and a corner complex whose cohomology computes the tangent and obstruction spaces. The virtual dimension formula receives a contribution from each corner equal to the dimension of the Levi subgroup determined by the residue of the Nahm pole. Examiners often ask for the explicit expression of the corner symplectic term, the proof that the total symplectic form is closed, and the identification of the Levi subgroup from the commuting residue condition. A common mistake is to treat the corner term as a boundary term that can be dropped by integration by parts; in fact it is essential for the symplectic reduction and for the correct index computation.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Boundary

The Vafa–Witten equations describe the topological twist of N=4 super Yang–Mills theory on a four‑manifold with a view toward S‑duality. When the manifold X has a non‑empty boundary ∂X, one must impose boundary conditions that preserve half of the supersymmetry. The standard choice is the Dirichlet condition for the gauge field and a Neumann‑type condition for the self‑dual two‑form field, which together define a Lagrangian submanifold in the space of boundary fields. The differential forms representing the symplectic structure on the moduli space M_VW(X) are obtained by evaluating the bulk action on solutions and adding a boundary term ∫∂X Tr(A ∧ δA) that makes the variational principle well‑posed. This boundary term is precisely the pullback of the Chern–Simons form and its exterior derivative yields the bulk symplectic current. The resulting moduli space carries a natural hyperkähler structure when X is a product Σ × I with Σ a Riemann surface; the boundary conditions then correspond to a choice of complex structure on Σ. The virtual dimension of M_VW(X) is given by the index of the linearised deformation operator with Atiyah–Patodi–Singer boundary conditions, and it can be expressed in terms of characteristic classes of the bundle and the eta invariant of the boundary Dirac operator. Examiners frequently test the derivation of the boundary term from the requirement of a well‑defined variational principle, the computation of the virtual dimension using the APS index theorem, and the identification of the hyperkähler moment maps associated to the boundary gauge symmetry. A typical trap is to confuse the Vafa–Witten boundary conditions with those of the Kapustin–Witten equations; the former involve a self‑dual two‑form while the latter involve a Higgs field, leading to different Lagrangian submanifolds in the boundary phase space.

Worked Example: Corner Contribution to the Kapustin–Witten Symplectic Form
Let M be a four‑manifold with a single corner C ≅ Σ × {0} where Σ is a closed oriented surface. A solution of the Kapustin–Witten equations near C has a Nahm‑pole singularity φ ∼ t-1 φ0 with φ0 ∈ 𝔤 constant and commuting with the residue of the gauge field. Show that the corner contribution to the symplectic form is ω_C = ∫_Σ Tr(φ0 ∧ δA_Σ) and verify that dω_C = 0 on the space of solutions modulo gauge.
The bulk symplectic current is ω_bulk = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δφ). Near the corner, write A = A_Σ + a_t dt, φ = t-1 φ0 + φ_reg. The variation δφ contains a term −t-2 φ0 δt which integrates to a boundary term after integration by parts. The singular part yields ∫_Σ Tr(φ0 ∧ δA_Σ) because δ(t-1 φ0) ∧ δA_Σ = −t-2 φ0 δt ∧ δA_Σ and the t‑integral produces the corner integral. Closure follows from the Bianchi identity d_A φ = 0 and the fact that φ0 is covariantly constant along Σ; thus dω_C = ∫_Σ Tr(d_A φ0 ∧ δA_Σ) = 0. The term is gauge invariant because φ0 commutes with the gauge transformation at the corner.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the symplectic form on a moduli space with corners or boundaries, first write the bulk Chern–Simons functional, then identify the boundary or corner terms required for a well‑posed variational principle. Remember that corner terms are not total derivatives; they encode the Levi subgroup data and must be kept. For index computations, always state the boundary condition type (APS, Dirichlet, Nahm‑pole) and cite the relevant index theorem (Atiyah–Patodi–Singer for boundaries, Melrose‑Piazza for corners).

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Calabi–Yau Fourfolds

Donaldson–Thomas theory on a Calabi–Yau fourfold X counts ideal sheaves of dimension two subschemes, or equivalently, stable pairs (F, s) with F a pure two‑dimensional sheaf and s a section. The virtual dimension of the moduli space M_DT(X) is zero because the obstruction theory is symmetric, a consequence of the trivial canonical bundle and Serre duality in dimension four. The perfect obstruction theory is built from the Atiyah class of the universal sheaf on X × M_DT(X) and yields a virtual fundamental class [M_DT(X)]^vir in the Chow group of dimension zero. The differential forms that appear in the localisation formula for the DT invariants are the equivariant Chern characters ch_T(E) of the universal sheaf E pushed forward to M_DT(X). These forms satisfy a holomorphic anomaly equation governed by the B‑model topological string on X. The generating series of DT invariants is expected to be the partition function of the topological string and to satisfy the wall‑crossing formula of Kontsevich–Soibelman across chambers of the stability manifold. Examiners often require the construction of the symmetric obstruction theory, the proof that the virtual dimension vanishes using the Calabi–Yau condition, and the statement of the wall‑crossing formula for the resolved conifold. A frequent error is to assume that the virtual class coincides with the ordinary fundamental class; it is only a cycle in the Chow group and its degree depends on the stability condition.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Boundary and Defects

Introducing surface defects in the Kapustin–Witten theory amounts to prescribing a singularity of the fields along a codimension‑two submanifold D ⊂ M. The defect is labelled by a representation of the gauge group and modifies the Nahm‑pole boundary condition on a tubular neighbourhood of D. The moduli space M_KW(M, D) carries a symplectic form that splits into a bulk part, a boundary part (if ∂M ≠ ∅), and a defect part ω_def = ∫_D Tr(φ_D ∧ δA_D) where φ_D is the residue of the Higgs field along D. The defect contribution is essential for the Hamiltonian action of the gauge group that preserves the defect data; it ensures that the moment map for this action is the curvature of the universal connection restricted to D. The deformation complex now includes a defect complex whose cohomology computes the tangent space to the moduli space of solutions with fixed defect type. The virtual dimension receives an additive contribution from each defect equal to the dimension of the corresponding coadjoint orbit. Examiners may ask for the explicit form of the defect symplectic term, the derivation of the moment map, and the modification of the index theorem in the presence of defects. A common pitfall is to treat the defect term as a boundary term on the complement of D; it is intrinsically supported on D and cannot be moved off the defect by integration by parts.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Haydys–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Boundary

The Haydys–Witten equations are a five‑dimensional gauge‑theoretic system whose solutions are expected to categorify the four‑dimensional Kapustin–Witten invariants. On a five‑manifold Y with boundary ∂Y, one imposes boundary conditions that preserve a topological supercharge; the natural choice is a Nahm‑pole type condition for the gauge field and a Dirichlet condition for the auxiliary one‑form. The moduli space M_HW(Y) is finite‑dimensional and carries a natural one‑form α = ∫_Y Tr(δA ∧ *F_A) whose exterior derivative gives a symplectic form on the interior. The boundary contribution to the symplectic structure is a Chern–Simons functional on ∂Y, and its variation yields the boundary moment map. The virtual dimension is computed by an index theorem for manifolds with boundary involving the eta invariant of the boundary Dirac operator coupled to the gauge field. The differential forms representing the cohomology classes on M_HW(Y) are constructed from the universal curvature and the boundary Chern–Simons form. Examiners often test the derivation of the boundary term from the variational principle, the identification of the boundary moment map, and the computation of the virtual dimension using the APS index theorem. A typical mistake is to omit the eta invariant contribution, which is crucial for the integrality of the virtual dimension.

  • Confusing the corner symplectic term in Kapustin–Witten theory with a boundary term that can be discarded by Stokes’ theorem.
  • Mixing the Vafa–Witten boundary conditions (self‑dual two‑form) with the Kapustin–Witten Nahm‑pole conditions (Higgs field).
  • Assuming the Donaldson–Thomas virtual class on a Calabi–Yau fourfold is an ordinary fundamental class rather than a cycle in the Chow group.
  • Treating the defect contribution in Kapustin–Witten theory as a boundary term on the complement of the defect submanifold.
  • Omitting the eta invariant in the APS index computation for the Haydys–Witten virtual dimension.
  • Neglecting the commuting residue condition at corners, which determines the Levi subgroup and the corner dimension formula.
  • Forgetting that the holomorphic anomaly equation governs the dependence of equivariant Chern characters on Kähler moduli in DT theory.

These advanced topics illustrate how differential forms encode symplectic structures, index theorems, and virtual fundamental classes across a spectrum of gauge‑theoretic moduli problems with boundaries, corners, and defects.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Calabi–Yau Fourfolds

The Donaldson–Thomas invariants on a Calabi–Yau fourfold X are defined via a symmetric obstruction theory on the moduli stack of ideal sheaves or stable pairs. Unlike the threefold case where the virtual dimension is zero by Serre duality, on a fourfold the virtual dimension remains zero but the obstruction theory is no longer self‑dual; instead one constructs a symmetric obstruction theory using the Atiyah class of the universal complex and the holomorphic volume form Ω ∈ H⁰(X, K_X). The resulting virtual class [M]ᵛⁱʳ lives in the Chow group A₀(M) and its degree yields the DT₄ invariant. A key technical point is the use of the Behrend function ν_M, which for a symmetric obstruction theory satisfies ν_M = (−1)dim M on the support of the virtual cycle. The wall‑crossing formula for DT₄ invariants under variation of stability condition was established by Cao–Leung–Toda (2019) and involves the Joyce–Song wall‑crossing structure on the Hall algebra of the derived category Dᵇ(Coh X). Examiners often ask for the explicit form of the symmetric obstruction map E → L_M[1] and the proof that the virtual dimension vanishes using the Calabi–Yau condition c₁(X)=0. A frequent error is to assume that the virtual class coincides with the ordinary fundamental class; it is only a cycle in the Chow group and its degree depends on the stability condition.

The deformation–obstruction complex for a stable pair (F, s) is given by the derived Hom complex RHom(I, I)₀[1] where I is the ideal sheaf. The symmetric pairing is induced by the trace map and the holomorphic volume form, yielding a non‑degenerate quadratic form on the obstruction space. The virtual fundamental class is then constructed via the intrinsic normal cone of Behrend–Fantechi, and its degree is computed by localization on the torus‑fixed locus when X admits a torus action. In the local toric CY₄ case, the DT₄ partition function can be expressed in terms of the topological vertex for fourfolds, a generalization of the threefold vertex due to Cao–Maulik–Toda. The holomorphic anomaly equation for DT₄ invariants, conjectured by Borisov–Joyce, governs the dependence of the generating series on the Kähler moduli and involves the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of X. Candidates should be able to derive the anomaly equation from the BCOV formalism and explain how the symmetric obstruction theory modifies the usual B‑model propagator.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Corners

The Kapustin–Witten equations are a topological twist of N=4 super‑Yang–Mills theory on a four‑manifold M with a parameter t ∈ ℂℙ¹. When M has corners of codimension two, the natural boundary conditions are Nahm‑pole type on each boundary hypersurface and a commuting residue condition at the corners. The moduli space M_KW(M) carries a symplectic form ω = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δφ) + ∑_H ∫_H Tr(δA_H ∧ δφ_H) + ∑_C ω_C where the sum runs over boundary hypersurfaces H and corners C. The corner term ω_C = ∫_C Tr(φ_C ∧ δA_C) is essential for the Hamiltonian action of the gauge group preserving the corner data; its moment map is the restriction of the curvature of the universal connection to the corner. The deformation complex now includes a corner complex whose cohomology computes the tangent space to the moduli space with fixed corner residues. The virtual dimension receives an additive contribution from each corner equal to the dimension of the corresponding coadjoint orbit of the Levi subgroup determined by the commuting residues. Examiners may ask for the explicit form of the corner symplectic term, the derivation of the moment map, and the modification of the index theorem in the presence of corners using the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer theorem with corner corrections. A common pitfall is to treat the corner term as a boundary term on the complement of the corner; it is intrinsically supported on the corner and cannot be moved off by integration by parts.

The Nahm‑pole boundary condition requires that near a boundary hypersurface H the fields behave as A = A_H + t⁻¹ α_H dt + … and φ = t⁻¹ β_H + … where α_H, β_H satisfy the Nahm equations on the interval. At a corner where two hypersurfaces meet, the residues β_H¹, β_H² must commute, defining a Levi subgroup L ⊂ G. The corner symplectic form is then the Kirillov–Kostant form on the coadjoint orbit O_λ ⊂ 𝔩* determined by the common eigenvalues. The virtual dimension formula reads vdim = 2 ∫_M c₂(ad P) − ½ dim G ⋅ χ(M) + ∑_H dim Oλ_H + ∑_C dim Oλ_C. The index theorem for the deformation operator with Nahm‑pole and corner conditions involves the eta invariant of the boundary Dirac operator coupled to the gauge field and a corner contribution given by the spectral flow of the family of Dirac operators along the corner. This spectral flow can be computed using the adiabatic limit of the eta form, a technique developed by Mazzeo–Melrose. Candidates should be able to set up the adiabatic limit and identify the corner contribution to the index.

Worked Example: Corner Contribution to the Virtual Dimension
Consider the Kapustin–Witten equations on a four‑manifold M = Σ × I × I where Σ is a Riemann surface and I = [0,1]. Impose Nahm‑pole boundary conditions on the two boundary hypersurfaces H₁ = Σ × {0} × I and H₂ = Σ × I × {0} with residues β₁, β₂ ∈ 𝔤 that commute. Compute the corner contribution to the virtual dimension at the corner C = Σ × {0} × {0}.
The commuting residues β₁, β₂ define a Levi subgroup L = Z_G(β₁, β₂). The corner symplectic form is the Kirillov–Kostant form on the coadjoint orbit O_λ ⊂ 𝔩* where λ is the common eigenvalue of β₁, β₂ under the identification 𝔩* ≅ 𝔩 via the Killing form. The dimension of O_λ equals dim G − dim L. Since the corner is a product Σ × {point}, the corner contribution to the virtual dimension is (dim G − dim L) ⋅ χ(Σ). For G = SU(2) and generic commuting residues, L = U(1) so dim G − dim L = 3 − 1 = 2. If Σ has genus g, χ(Σ) = 2−2g, giving a corner contribution 2(2−2g). This matches the general formula vdim_corner = ∑_C dim Oλ_C ⋅ χ(C).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for the virtual dimension of a moduli space with corners, first list all boundary hypersurfaces and corners. Write down the Nahm‑pole residues on each hypersurface, then impose the commuting condition at each corner to determine the Levi subgroup. The corner contribution is always the dimension of the corresponding coadjoint orbit times the Euler characteristic of the corner. Do not forget the bulk term 2∫ c₂(ad P) and the boundary eta invariants; the APS index theorem with corners adds a spectral‑flow term that can be evaluated by the adiabatic limit of the eta form.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Haydys–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Corners

The Haydys–Witten equations are a five‑dimensional gauge‑theoretic system whose solutions are expected to categorify the four‑dimensional Kapustin–Witten invariants. On a five‑manifold Y with corners of codimension two, one imposes Nahm‑pole type boundary conditions on each boundary hypersurface and a commuting residue condition at the corners, analogous to the four‑dimensional case. The moduli space M_HW(Y) is finite‑dimensional and carries a natural one‑form α = ∫_Y Tr(δA ∧ *F_A) whose exterior derivative gives a symplectic form on the interior. The boundary contribution to the symplectic structure is a Chern–Simons functional on each boundary hypersurface, and its variation yields the boundary moment map. At a corner, the moment maps from the two adjacent hypersurfaces must agree, which forces the residues to commute and defines a Levi subgroup. The virtual dimension is computed by an index theorem for manifolds with corners involving the eta invariant of the boundary Dirac operator coupled to the gauge field and a corner contribution given by the spectral flow of the family of Dirac operators along the corner. The differential forms representing the cohomology classes on M_HW(Y) are constructed from the universal curvature and the boundary Chern–Simons form, together with a corner term that is the transgression of the Chern–Simons form to the corner. Examiners often test the derivation of the boundary term from the variational principle, the identification of the boundary moment map, and the computation of the virtual dimension using the APS index theorem with corner corrections. A typical mistake is to omit the eta invariant contribution, which is crucial for the integrality of the virtual dimension.

The deformation complex for the Haydys–Witten equations with corners is a three‑term complex Ω⁰(ad P) → Ω¹(ad P) ⊕ Ω⁰(ad P) → Ω²(ad P) ⊕ Ω¹(ad P) where the maps are the linearized equations. The Nahm‑pole boundary condition imposes a specific asymptotic behavior on the fields near each boundary hypersurface, and the corner condition requires that the leading order residues commute. The index of this complex with the given boundary conditions is given by the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer formula for manifolds with corners, which includes a bulk integral of the Chern character, a boundary integral of the eta form, and a corner integral of the transgressed eta form. The corner term can be evaluated using the adiabatic limit of the eta form as the collar neighborhoods of the boundaries shrink, a technique developed by Melrose–Piazza. Candidates should be able to write down the adiabatic limit formula and explain how the corner contribution reduces to the dimension of the coadjoint orbit of the Levi subgroup determined by the commuting residues.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Boundary

The Vafa–Witten equations are a topological twist of N=4 super‑Yang–Mills theory on a four‑manifold M with a parameter τ in the upper half‑plane. When M has a boundary ∂M, the natural boundary conditions preserving the topological supercharge are a Dirichlet condition for the gauge field A and a Neumann condition for the self‑dual two‑form B, together with a Nahm‑pole condition for the scalar φ. The moduli space M_VW(M) carries a symplectic form ω = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δB) + ∫_∂M Tr(δA_∂ ∧ δφ_∂) where the boundary term is the pullback of the bulk symplectic form to the boundary. The boundary contribution is essential for the Hamiltonian action of the gauge group that preserves the boundary data; its moment map is the restriction of the curvature of the universal connection to ∂M. The deformation complex includes a boundary complex whose cohomology computes the tangent space to the moduli space with fixed boundary values. The virtual dimension receives an additive contribution from the boundary equal to the dimension of the moduli space of flat connections on ∂M with the prescribed Nahm‑pole residue. Examiners may ask for the explicit form of the boundary symplectic term, the derivation of the moment map, and the modification of the index theorem in the presence of boundary using the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer theorem. A common pitfall is to confuse the Vafa–Witten boundary conditions (self‑dual two‑form) with the Kapustin–Witten Nahm‑pole conditions (Higgs field).

The Vafa–Witten equations read F_A⁺ = [B, B]⁺, d_A* B = 0, d_A φ = 0 together with the Bianchi identity. The topological supercharge Q squares to a gauge transformation generated by φ. The boundary conditions are chosen so that the Q‑exact action has no boundary variation: A|_∂M = A_∂ fixed, *B|_∂M = 0, and φ has a Nahm‑pole singularity φ ∼ t⁻¹ β near the boundary where t is a boundary defining function. The boundary symplectic form is ω_∂ = ∫_∂M Tr(δA_∂ ∧ δβ). The moment map for the gauge group action on the boundary is μ_∂ = FA_∂ + [β, β*]. The virtual dimension formula is vdim = 2 ∫_M c₂(ad P) − ½ dim G ⋅ χ(M) + dim M_flat(∂M, β) where M_flat(∂M, β) is the moduli space of flat connections on ∂M with holonomy determined by β. The index theorem for the deformation operator with these boundary conditions involves the eta invariant of the boundary Dirac operator coupled to the gauge field and the boundary connection A_∂. Candidates should be able to derive the boundary term from the variational principle and compute the eta invariant contribution for a simple case such as M = Σ × I with product boundary conditions.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Bogomolny Equations on Manifolds with Boundary

The Bogomolny equations F_A = *d_A φ describe magnetic monopoles on a three‑manifold M. When M has a boundary ∂M, the natural boundary conditions preserving the hyperkähler structure of the moduli space are a Dirichlet condition for the Higgs field φ|_∂M = φ_∂ and a Neumann condition for the gauge field *F_A|_∂M = 0, together with a Nahm‑pole singularity for φ near the boundary if one wants to include singular monopoles. The moduli space M_Bog(M) carries a hyperkähler metric constructed from the L² metric on the space of solutions. The symplectic forms ω_I, ω_J, ω_K are given by ω_I = ∫_M Tr(δA ∧ δφ) + ∫_∂M Tr(δA_∂ ∧ δφ_∂) and cyclic permutations. The boundary term is crucial for the tri‑Hamiltonian action of the gauge group preserving the boundary data; its moment maps are the restrictions of the three curvature components to ∂M. The deformation complex is a two‑term complex Ω⁰(ad P) → Ω¹(ad P) ⊕ Ω⁰(ad P) with the linearized Bogomolny operator. The virtual dimension is given by the index of this operator with the chosen boundary conditions, which can be computed via the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with boundary. The boundary contribution involves the eta invariant of the boundary Dirac operator coupled to the gauge field and the boundary value φ_∂. Examiners often test the derivation of the boundary symplectic term, the identification of the three moment maps, and the computation of the virtual dimension using the APS index theorem. A typical mistake is to neglect the boundary term in the symplectic form, which leads to an incorrect moment map and a failure of the hyperkähler quotient construction.

For M = Σ × I with Σ a Riemann surface, the Bogomolny equations reduce to the Hitchin equations on Σ. The boundary conditions at the two ends of the interval correspond to Nahm‑pole singularities with residues β₀, β₁ ∈ 𝔤. The moduli space of solutions is then the moduli space of Higgs bundles on Σ with prescribed singularities at the punctures. The hyperkähler metric on this moduli space is the L² metric, and its Kähler forms are given by the above formulas with boundary terms at the punctures. The virtual dimension equals 2 dim M_Higgs(Σ, β₀, β₁) where M_Higgs is the Hitchin moduli space. The index theorem for the deformation operator on Σ × I with Nahm‑pole ends reduces to the Riemann–Roch theorem for the Hitchin moduli space, and the boundary eta invariants cancel between the two ends. Candidates should be able to set up the Nahm transform for monopoles on Σ × I and explain how the boundary conditions translate into parabolic structures on the Higgs bundle.

Common Traps

  • Assuming the virtual class in Donaldson–Thomas theory on a Calabi–Yau fourfold coincides with the ordinary fundamental class; it is only a cycle in the Chow group and its degree depends on the stability condition.
  • Treating the corner symplectic term in Kapustin–Witten theory as a boundary term on the complement of the corner; it is intrinsically supported on the corner and cannot be moved off by integration by parts.
  • Omitting the eta invariant contribution in the APS index computation for the Haydys–Witten virtual dimension; the eta invariant is crucial for integrality.
  • Confusing the Vafa–Witten boundary conditions (self‑dual two‑form) with the Kapustin–Witten Nahm‑pole conditions (Higgs field).
  • Neglecting the commuting residue condition at corners, which determines the Levi subgroup and the corner dimension formula.
  • Forgetting that the holomorphic anomaly equation governs the dependence of equivariant Chern characters on Kähler moduli in DT theory.
  • Overlooking the boundary term in the Bogomolny symplectic form, which leads to an incorrect moment map and a failure of the hyperkähler quotient construction.

The Classical de Rham Theorem: Sheaf Cohomology and Smooth Singular Cochains

The de Rham theorem asserts that the de Rham cohomology of a smooth manifold M is canonically isomorphic to its singular cohomology with real coefficients. The modern proof uses the sheaf-theoretic Leray theorem: the de Rham complex Ω•M is a fine resolution of the constant sheaf ℝM, so its hypercohomology computes sheaf cohomology H•(M, ℝ). Since fine sheaves are acyclic, the spectral sequence degenerates at E2, yielding HkdR(M) ≅ Hk(M, ℝ). An alternative elementary proof constructs a chain map I : Ω•(M) → C•∞(M; ℝ) to smooth singular cochains by integration over smooth simplices, and a homotopy inverse J using Whitney forms. The composition J ∘ I is chain homotopic to the identity via the homotopy operator K defined by Kω(σ) = ∫Δk×[0,1] H*ω where H : Δk × [0,1] → M is a smooth homotopy. The key technical point is that smooth singular chains compute the same cohomology as continuous ones because smooth approximation is a chain homotopy equivalence. Examiners often ask for the explicit formula of the homotopy operator K or the proof that the integration map induces an isomorphism on cohomology. The isomorphism is natural with respect to smooth maps and compatible with cup products: under the de Rham isomorphism, the wedge product corresponds to the cup product in singular cohomology. This compatibility is proved by showing that the Alexander-Whitney diagonal approximation and the wedge product are homotopic at the cochain level.

The Hodge Decomposition Theorem on Compact Riemannian Manifolds

On a compact oriented Riemannian manifold (M, g) of dimension n, the Hodge star operator ★ : Ωk → Ωn−k satisfies ★2 = (−1)k(n−k). The codifferential δ = (−1)n(k+1)+1 ★ d ★ is the formal adjoint of d with respect to the L2 inner product ⟨α, β⟩ = ∫M α ∧ ★β. The Hodge Laplacian Δ = dδ + δd is an elliptic, self-adjoint, non-negative operator. Hodge's theorem states that every de Rham cohomology class contains a unique harmonic representative: HkdR(M) ≅ ℋk(M) = {ω ∈ Ωk | Δω = 0}. The proof uses elliptic regularity to show that Δ has finite-dimensional kernel and closed range, then the orthogonal decomposition Ωk = ℋk ⊕ im d ⊕ im δ. The Green's operator G (the inverse of Δ on (ker Δ)⊥) yields the Hodge decomposition ω = Hω + dδGω + δdGω where H is the orthogonal projection onto ℋk. The heat kernel proof constructs e−tΔ via the Minakshisundaram-Pleijel asymptotic expansion e−tΔ(x, y) ∼ (4πt)−n/2 e−d(x,y)2/4t ∑j≥0 tj Φj(x, y) and shows that as t → ∞, e−tΔ converges to the projection H. The coefficients Φj are universal polynomials in the curvature tensor and its covariant derivatives; Φ0 = 1 and the integral of Φn/2 gives the Euler characteristic via the Chern-Gauss-Bonnet theorem. Candidates must be able to derive the Weitzenböck formula Δ = ∇*∇ + ℛ where ℛ is a curvature endomorphism, and apply it to prove vanishing theorems (e.g., Bochner's theorem: if Ric > 0 then H1 = 0).

Worked Problem: Hodge Decomposition on the 2-Torus
Let M = T2 = ℝ2/ℤ2 with the flat metric ds2 = dx2 + dy2. Compute the Hodge decomposition of the 1-form ω = x dy (where x is the coordinate on the universal cover). Identify its harmonic part, exact part, and coexact part.
On T2, the harmonic 1-forms are spanned by dx and dy. The form ω = x dy is not well-defined on T2 because x is not periodic. However, its exterior derivative dω = dx ∧ dy is well-defined. To make ω well-defined, we must add a correction: consider ω = x dy − ½ dx ∧ dy? No, that is a 2-form. The correct approach: ω is not a global form. Instead, take a well-defined 1-form like ω = sin(2πx) dy. Then dω = 2π cos(2πx) dx ∧ dy, δω = −★d★ω = −★d(sin(2πx) dx) = 0. So ω is coexact? No, δω = 0 means ω is coclosed. The Hodge decomposition: ω = Hω + dα + δβ. Since δω = 0, ω has no coexact part. The harmonic projection Hω = (∫T2 ω ∧ ★dx) dx + (∫T2 ω ∧ ★dy) dy = 0 because ∫ sin(2πx) dx = 0. Thus ω is exact: ω = dα with α = −(1/2π) cos(2πx). Verify: dα = sin(2πx) dx? No, dα = sin(2πx) dx, not dy. Correction: ω = sin(2πx) dy = d(−(1/2π) cos(2πx) y)? That is not periodic in y. The harmonic part is zero, the exact part is zero (since ω is not exact on T2 — its integral over the y-cycle is non-zero for some x), and the coexact part is ω itself? Wait: δω = 0, so ω is coclosed. On a compact manifold, a coclosed form is coexact iff it is orthogonal to harmonic forms. Since Hω = 0, ω is coexact: ω = δβ for some 2-form β. Let β = f dx ∧ dy. Then δβ = −★d★β = −★df = −★(fx dx + fy dy) = −fx dy + fy dx. Set equal to sin(2πx) dy: fy = 0, −fx = sin(2πx) ⇒ f = (1/2π) cos(2πx). So β = (1/2π) cos(2πx) dx ∧ dy and ω = δβ. Decomposition: ω = 0 + 0 + δβ.
Exam Strategy: Hodge Theory Computations
When asked to compute Hodge decompositions on flat tori or spheres, always first identify the harmonic forms (constant coefficient forms on Tn, only 0 and n-forms on Sn). Use Fourier series: expand the form in eigenforms of Δ. The harmonic part is the zero-frequency mode. The exact part comes from d of the potential obtained by dividing non-zero Fourier coefficients by the eigenvalue. The coexact part comes from δ of the copotential. Remember that on Tn, Δ = −∑ ∂i2 on functions, and on k-forms it acts componentwise. The Green's operator G is simply division by the eigenvalue on each non-zero Fourier mode.

The Chern-Weil Homomorphism and Explicit Characteristic Classes

The Chern-Weil homomorphism constructs characteristic classes of a principal G-bundle P → M with connection A and curvature F = dA + ½[A, A]. For any Ad-invariant polynomial P on the Lie algebra 𝔤, the form P(F) is closed and its cohomology class is independent of the connection. The map P ↦ [P(F)] defines a homomorphism from the algebra of invariant polynomials I(𝔤)G to H•(M, ℝ). For G = U(n), the elementary symmetric polynomials in the eigenvalues of iF/2π give the Chern forms: ck(F) = (1/k!) (i/2π)k Tr(Fk) for the total Chern class c = det(I + iF/2π). The first Chern form is c1 = (i/2π) Tr F; the second is c2 = (1/8π2)[(Tr F)2 − Tr(F2)]. For G = SO(n) or Sp(n), the Pontryagin forms are pk = (−1)k c2k of the complexified bundle. The Euler form for an oriented rank-n bundle with connection is e(F) = Pf(F/2π) where Pf is the Pfaffian; for n = 2, e = (1/2π) F12; for n = 4, e = (1/32π2) εijkl Fij Fkl. The Chern-Simons forms provide transgression: if At = tA1 + (1−t)A0 is a path of connections, then P(F1) − P(F0) = d CSP(A0, A1) where CSP = k ∫01 P(A1 − A0, Ft, …, Ft) dt. For P(X) = Tr(Xk), the Chern-Simons form is CSk = k ∫01 Tr((A1 − A0) Ftk−1) dt. Examiners test the derivation of the Bianchi identity dAF = 0 and the proof that d P(F) = 0 using invariance of P. A common question: show that the Chern-Weil form of a flat connection is exact (it is d of the Chern-Simons form relative to the trivial connection).

The Thom Isomorphism, Euler Class, and the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern Theorem

Let π : E → M be an oriented rank-n vector bundle with a metric and compatible connection. The Thom class ΦE ∈ Hnc(E) is a compactly supported cohomology class characterized by ∫Ex ΦE = 1 for each fiber Ex. The Thom isomorphism Φ : Hk(M) → Hk+nc(E) is Φ(α) = π*α ∧ ΦE. In de Rham theory, ΦE is represented by a compactly supported form τ constructed via the Mathai-Quillen formalism: τ = (1/(2π)n/2) e−|v|2/2 ∑k≥0 (1/k!) ψi1 … ψik Ri1j1 … Rikjk vj1 … vjk where v are fiber coordinates and ψ are Grassmann variables. The Euler class e(E) = s*ΦE where s : M → E is the zero section. For the tangent bundle TM of a compact oriented manifold, the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern theorem states χ(M) = ∫M e(TM). The Pfaffian formula e = Pf(R/2π) gives the integrand. For n = 2, e = (1/2π) K dA where K is the Gaussian curvature. For n = 4, e = (1/32π2) (|R|2 − 4|Ric|2 + R2) dvol. The proof uses the Thom class of the normal bundle of the diagonal M → M × M and the Lefschetz fixed point formula for the identity map. Alternatively, one constructs a vector field with isolated zeros and uses the Poincaré-Hopf theorem: χ(M) = ∑p indp(V). The index is computed by the degree of the map V/|V| : Sn−1 → Sn−1 around each zero, which equals the integral of the Euler form over a small ball. Candidates must know the relation between the Euler class and the top Chern class: for a complex vector bundle, e(Eℝ) = cn(E). The Gysin sequence Hk−n(M) → Hk(M) → Hk(S(E)) → Hk−n+1(M) relates the cohomology of the sphere bundle to that of the base.

The Atiyah-Singer Index Theorem: Heat Kernel Proof and Local Index Density

The Atiyah-Singer index theorem for a Dirac-type operator D = d + d* on a compact manifold states ind D = ∫M â(TM) ∧ ch(E/S) where â is the Â-genus and ch is the Chern character. The heat kernel proof considers the supertrace Str e−tD2 = ∑λ (−1)f(λ) e−tλ where f is the fermion number. As t → ∞, only zero modes contribute, giving ind D. As t → 0, the Minakshisundaram-Pleijel expansion Str e−tD2(x, x) ∼ (4πt)−n/2 ∑j≥0 tj aj(x) yields the local index density an/2(x). The Getzler rescaling technique computes an/2 by assigning weight 1 to t, weight 1/2 to x, weight 1 to ∂x, and weight 0 to curvature. The rescaled operator Dt converges to the harmonic oscillator on the tangent space, whose supertrace gives the Â-genus. For the signature operator D = d + d* on Ω•, the index is the signature σ(M) and the local density is the Hirzebruch L-polynomial in the Pontryagin forms. For the spinc Dirac operator, the index is the Â-genus twisted by the determinant line bundle. The local index theorem for families (Bismut-Freed) states that the curvature of the determinant line bundle with the Quillen metric is the degree-2 component of ∫M/B â(TM/B) ch(E). Examiners often ask for the computation of the first few Seeley-deWitt coefficients: a0 = 1, a1 = (1/6) R − E where E is the endomorphism in the Weitzenböck formula Δ = −gij∇i∇j + E. For the Hodge Laplacian on k-forms, E is the Weitzenböck curvature term. The index density for the Euler characteristic is the Pfaffian of the curvature, recovering Gauss-Bonnet-Chern.

  • Confusing the Chern-Weil homomorphism (which produces real characteristic classes) with the integral characteristic classes in H•(M, ℤ); the Chern-Weil forms represent the image of integral classes under the map H•(M, ℤ) → H•(M, ℝ).
  • Forgetting that the Thom class lives in compactly supported cohomology Hnc(E), not ordinary cohomology; the Thom isomorphism maps to H•+nc(E).
  • Misapplying the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern theorem to non-compact manifolds without boundary terms; the theorem requires compactness or suitable decay conditions.
  • Confusing the Â-genus (for the Dirac operator) with the L-genus (for the signature operator); they differ by a factor of 2n/2 in dimension n = 4k.
  • Neglecting the fermion number grading in the supertrace for the heat kernel proof; the supertrace is essential for cancellation of non-zero modes.
  • Assuming the Chern-Simons form is globally defined on the manifold; it is only defined locally or on the total space of the bundle, and its ambiguity under gauge transformations gives the Wess-Zumino-Witten term.
  • Misidentifying the Euler class of a complex bundle as the top Chern class of the underlying real bundle; the correct relation is e(Eℝ) = cn(E) for a complex rank-n bundle.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Nahm Equations and the Nahm Transform

The Nahm equations constitute a system of ordinary differential equations for matrix-valued functions on an interval, arising as the dimensional reduction of the anti-self-dual Yang–Mills equations on ℝ⁴ to one dimension. For a compact Lie group G with Lie algebra 𝔤, the Nahm data consist of three 𝔤-valued functions T₁(s), T₂(s), T₃(s) on an interval I ⊂ ℝ satisfying dTᵢ/ds = ½ εᵢⱼₖ [Tⱼ, Tₖ]. These equations are the compatibility condition for a Lax pair and define an integrable system. The moduli space of solutions with prescribed singular behavior at the endpoints of I is hyperkähler, carrying a natural hyperkähler metric constructed via the Nahm transform. The Nahm transform establishes a duality between solutions of the Nahm equations on an interval and solutions of the Bogomolny equations (monopoles) on ℝ³. Given Nahm data (T₁, T₂, T₃) on [0, 2] with simple poles at the endpoints and residues forming an irreducible representation of 𝔰𝔲(2), one constructs a rank-k monopole on ℝ³ by solving the Weyl equation (d/ds + i x·T(s)) Ψ = 0 for normalizable sections Ψ. The monopole fields are then recovered as Aᵢ = ∫ Ψ† ∂ᵢ Ψ ds and Φ = ∫ Ψ† T₀ Ψ ds where T₀ is the fourth Nahm matrix. The hyperkähler metric on the moduli space of monopoles coincides with the L² metric on the space of Nahm data. The differential forms on the Nahm moduli space are generated by the Atiyah–Hitchin symplectic forms ωᵢ = ∫ Tr(δTⱼ ∧ δTₖ) ds. The Nahm transform extends to monopoles with singularities (ramified monopoles) and to periodic monopoles (calorons), where the interval becomes a circle and the Nahm data satisfy periodic boundary conditions. The caloron moduli space is isomorphic to the moduli space of instantons on ℝ³ × S¹, and the Nahm transform realizes the Fourier–Mukai transform on the level of differential forms. The ADHM construction for instantons on ℝ⁴ has a Nahm-type description via the ADHM–Nahm transform, where the Nahm data are replaced by solutions of the Nahm equations on a collection of intervals corresponding to the Dynkin diagram of the gauge group. The hyperkähler quotient construction of the moduli space identifies it with the space of solutions to the Nahm equations modulo gauge transformations, and the moment maps for the hyperkähler action are precisely the Nahm equations themselves. The twistor space of the Nahm moduli space is the space of solutions to the linearized Nahm equations, and the Penrose–Ward transform relates differential forms on the moduli space to cohomology classes on the twistor space. The Nahm equations also appear in the description of M-theory fivebranes ending on M-theory ninebranes, where the Nahm pole boundary condition corresponds to a fuzzy sphere geometry. The differential forms on the moduli space of Nahm data with poles are related to the intersection cohomology of the Uhlenbeck compactification of the monopole moduli space. The L² cohomology of the Nahm moduli space carries a natural action of the mapping class group of the interval with marked points, and the resulting representation is related to the geometric Langlands correspondence for the group G. The Nahm transform for higher-rank gauge groups involves the Langlands dual group Ǧ, and the differential forms on the moduli space transform under the action of the affine Weyl group of Ǧ. The hyperkähler metric on the Nahm moduli space admits a complete asymptotic expansion in terms of the monopole separation, and the leading term is the semi-flat metric on the Hitchin fibration. The subleading corrections are governed by the BPS spectrum of the associated N=2 supersymmetric gauge theory, and the differential forms encoding these corrections are constructed from the spectral network of the Hitchin system.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Hitchin Equations with Irregular Singularities and Wild Character Varieties

The Hitchin equations on a compact Riemann surface Σ with irregular singularities at marked points p₁,…,pₙ generalize the standard Hitchin system by allowing the Higgs field Φ to have poles of order greater than one. Near a marked point p with local coordinate z, the Higgs field takes the form Φ = (A₋ₖ/zᵏ + … + A₋₁/z + A₀ + …) dz where k ≥ 2 is the order of the pole. The leading coefficient A₋ₖ is required to be regular semisimple, defining a spectral curve Σ̃ ⊂ T*Σ with an irregular singularity. The moduli space of solutions M_H(Σ, G, {kᵢ}) carries a natural hyperkähler structure, and its differential forms are generated by the hyperkähler symplectic forms ωᵢ and the tautological classes coming from the universal bundle. The wild character variety M_B(Σ, G, {kᵢ}) is the moduli space of meromorphic connections on a principal G-bundle over Σ with prescribed irregular singularities, modulo gauge transformations that are the identity at the marked points. The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence extends to this setting, giving a diffeomorphism M_H ≅ M_B that is not holomorphic but preserves the hyperkähler structure. The differential forms on M_B are generated by the Goldman symplectic form and the characteristic classes of the monodromy representation. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert map sends the Higgs field Φ to the Stokes data of the meromorphic connection ∇ = d + Φ + Φ†. The Stokes matrices S₁,…,Sₘ satisfy the wild monodromy relation M_formal S₁ … Sₘ = 1 where M_formal is the formal monodromy. The moduli space of Stokes data carries a natural Poisson structure, and the symplectic leaves are the wild character varieties. The isomonodromic deformation equations for irregular connections are the Painlevé equations (for rank 2 on ℙ¹ with one irregular singularity of order 2) or their higher-rank generalizations. The τ-function of the isomonodromic system generates the differential forms on the moduli space via the Jimbo–Miwa–Ueno formula d log τ = ½ Tr(Φ dΦ) + …. The wild character variety admits a cluster algebra structure, and the differential forms corresponding to cluster variables are the pullbacks of the Goldman symplectic form under the coordinate charts. The quantization of the wild character variety yields the quantum cluster algebra, and the differential forms on the classical moduli space are the semiclassical limits of the quantum cluster mutations. The wild Hitchin fibration M_H → B_wild has generic fibers that are abelian varieties (generalized Jacobians of the spectral curve), and the differential forms on the base B_wild are the invariant polynomials of the irregular Higgs field. The irregular Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the meromorphic connection is related to the weight filtration on the wild character variety via the irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence. The Stokes phenomenon for the Hitchin equations is governed by the wall-crossing formula for the BPS invariants of the associated N=2 theory, and the differential forms encoding the wall-crossing are the Kontsevich–Soibelman symplectomorphisms acting on the wild character variety. The wild non-abelian Hodge correspondence identifies the Dolbeault moduli space of irregular Higgs bundles with the de Rham moduli space of irregular connections and the Betti moduli space of wild representations. The differential forms on these three moduli spaces are related by the twistor space construction, where the twistor space is a hyperkähler manifold fibered over ℙ¹ with fibers the three moduli spaces. The irregular Hodge numbers of the wild character variety are computed by the mixed Hodge structure on the cohomology of the moduli stack of irregular connections, and the generating function of these Hodge numbers is the wild character variety analogue of the Hausel–Letellier–Rodriguez-Villegas formula. The wild character variety for G = SL(2, ℂ) with one irregular singularity of order k on ℙ¹ is isomorphic to the moduli space of solutions to the k-th Painlevé equation, and the differential forms on this space are the Okamoto symplectic forms. The quantization of the wild character variety for higher-rank groups is related to the representation theory of the quantum group U_q(𝔤) at a root of unity, and the differential forms on the classical moduli space correspond to the semiclassical limit of the quantum group characters.

Worked Problem: Nahm Transform for SU(2) Monopoles
Consider the Nahm data for a charge-2 SU(2) monopole: Tᵢ(s) = (i/2) σᵢ / (s-1) for i=1,2,3 on the interval (0,2), where σᵢ are the Pauli matrices. Verify that this satisfies the Nahm equations and construct the corresponding monopole fields on ℝ³ by solving the Weyl equation.
The Nahm equations are dTᵢ/ds = ½ εᵢⱼₖ [Tⱼ, Tₖ]. For Tᵢ = (i/2) σᵢ/(s-1), we have dTᵢ/ds = -(i/2) σᵢ/(s-1)². The commutator [Tⱼ, Tₖ] = (i/2)² [σⱼ, σₖ]/(s-1)² = -(1/4) (2i εⱼₖₗ σₗ)/(s-1)² = -(i/2) εⱼₖₗ σₗ/(s-1)². Then ½ εᵢⱼₖ [Tⱼ, Tₖ] = ½ εᵢⱼₖ (-(i/2) εⱼₖₗ σₗ/(s-1)²) = -(i/4) (2 δᵢₗ) σₗ/(s-1)² = -(i/2) σᵢ/(s-1)² = dTᵢ/ds. The Weyl equation is (d/ds + i x·T(s)) Ψ = 0. With x·T = (i/2) (x·σ)/(s-1), this becomes dΨ/ds - (1/2) (x·σ)/(s-1) Ψ = 0. The solution is Ψ(s) = (s-1)^(x·σ/2) Ψ₀. Normalizability requires Re(eigenvalues of x·σ/2) < 0, which holds for |x| > 0 with appropriate Ψ₀. The monopole fields are Aᵢ = ∫ Ψ† ∂ᵢ Ψ ds and Φ = ∫ Ψ† T₀ Ψ ds where T₀ = 0 for this solution. The resulting fields are the charge-2 axially symmetric monopole with Higgs field Φ = (|x| coth|x| - 1) (x·σ)/|x|² and gauge potential Aᵢ = (1 - |x|/sinh|x|) εᵢⱼₖ xⱼ σₖ/|x|².
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Nahm–Hitchin Duality
When a question asks for the moduli space metric of monopoles or instantons, check whether the problem is set on ℝ³ (monopoles → Nahm transform on an interval) or ℝ⁴ (instantons → ADHM construction). The Nahm transform converts the nonlinear Bogomolny PDE on ℝ³ to the Nahm ODE on an interval. The hyperkähler metric on the monopole moduli space is the L² metric on Nahm data. For calorons (periodic monopoles), the interval becomes a circle and the Nahm data are periodic. The ADHM–Nahm transform for instantons on ℝ⁴ uses Nahm data on a quiver of intervals. Remember: Nahm equations = dimensional reduction of anti-self-dual Yang–Mills to 1D; Hitchin equations = dimensional reduction to 2D. The wild Hitchin system adds irregular singularities (poles of order ≥2) and corresponds to isomonodromic deformations (Painlevé equations). The wild character variety carries a cluster algebra structure; its quantization gives quantum cluster algebras.

Differential Forms and the Theory of the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations with Ramification and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The Kapustin–Witten equations on a four-manifold M = Σ × C, where Σ is a Riemann surface and C is a curve, are a family of equations interpolating between the Hitchin equations on Σ and the anti-self-dual Yang–Mills equations on M. With ramification at points p₁,…,pₙ ∈ Σ, the gauge field A and the Higgs field Φ are allowed to have prescribed singularities modeled on the nilpotent orbits of the Langlands dual group Ǧ. The ramification data consist of a choice of Levi subgroup L ⊂ G at each puncture and a nilpotent element e ∈ 𝔩. The moduli space of solutions M_KW(Σ, C, G, {ramification}) carries a natural hyperkähler structure when C is a cylinder, and its differential forms are generated by the hyperkähler symplectic forms and the characteristic classes of the universal bundle. The geometric Langlands correspondence predicts an equivalence between the derived category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles on Σ with ramification and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of Ǧ-local systems on Σ with dual ramification. The Kapustin–Witten equations provide a physical realization of this correspondence via S-duality of N=4 super Yang–Mills theory. The differential forms on the moduli space of ramified Kapustin–Witten solutions are related to the Hecke eigensheaf property: a Hecke eigensheaf on the moduli stack of G-bundles corresponds to a Ǧ-local system, and the eigenvalue of the Hecke operator is the fiber of the local system. The ramification introduces 't Hooft operators in the gauge theory, which are disorder operators creating prescribed singularities. The differential forms on the moduli space of solutions with 't Hooft operators are the correlation functions of these operators in the topological twist of N=4 SYM. The S-duality transformation exchanges 't Hooft operators for Wilson operators, and the differential forms transform under the action of the modular group SL(2, ℤ) on the coupling constant τ = θ/2π + 4πi/g². The ramified geometric Langlands correspondence for G = SL(2) and Ǧ = PGL(2) relates the moduli space of rank-2 bundles with parabolic structures to the moduli space of PGL(2)-local systems with irregular singularities. The differential forms on the parabolic moduli space are the pullbacks of the Goldman symplectic form under the forgetful map to the character variety. The quantization of the Kapustin–Witten equations with ramification yields the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, where the differential forms on the classical moduli space are replaced by categories of D-modules. The categorical action of the braid group on the derived category of D-modules is generated by the Hecke functors, and the differential forms on the moduli space of solutions encode the monodromy of the KZ connection. The ramified Kapustin–Witten equations on M = Σ × ℝ² with boundary conditions at ℝ² = {0} × ℝ² give rise to the boundary geometric Langlands correspondence, where the differential forms on the moduli space of solutions with boundary are related to the category of boundary conditions in the topological field theory. The boundary conditions are labeled by branes in the Hitchin moduli space: (B,A,A)-branes correspond to D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles, and (B,B,B)-branes correspond to coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of Ǧ-local systems. The differential forms on the moduli space of solutions with boundary are the open-string amplitudes between these branes. The ramification data for the boundary theory are encoded in the choice of nilpotent orbit for the Nahm pole boundary condition, and the differential forms on the moduli space of Nahm poles are the intersection cohomology forms of the nilpotent orbit closure. The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with ramification is expected to be an equivalence of 2-categories, and the differential forms on the moduli space of Kapustin–Witten solutions are the 2-morphisms in this 2-category. The verification of this correspondence for specific ramification data involves the computation of the differential forms on the moduli space of solutions using the localization formula for the torus action on the Hitchin moduli space, and the comparison with the differential forms on the moduli space of Ǧ-local systems using the irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations on Four-Manifolds with b₂⁺ = 1 and Wall-Crossing Phenomena

The Vafa–Witten equations on a closed oriented four-manifold X are a set of equations for a connection A on a principal G-bundle and a self-dual two-form B ∈ Ω²₊(X, ad P) together with a scalar field φ ∈ Ω⁰(X, ad P): F₊(A) = [B, B] + dₐ*φ, dₐB = 0, dₐ*dₐφ = [B, *B]. These equations arise from the topological twist of N=4 super Yang–Mills theory and are invariant under the action of the modular group SL(2, ℤ). The moduli space of solutions M_VW(X, G) carries a virtual fundamental class, and the Vafa–Witten invariants are the integrals of differential forms over this virtual class. When b₂⁺(X) = 1, the moduli space depends on the choice of a chamber in the space of self-dual harmonic forms H²₊(X, ℝ), and the invariants jump across walls where the moduli space becomes non-compact due to the appearance of reducible solutions. The wall-crossing formula for the Vafa–Witten invariants is governed by the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall-crossing formula for the BPS invariants of the associated N=2 theory. The differential forms on the moduli space that encode the wall-crossing are the Euler classes of the obstruction bundles over the strata of reducible solutions. For G = SU(2), the reducible solutions correspond to splittings of the bundle as a sum of line bundles, and the wall-crossing is computed by the blow-up formula for the Vafa–Witten invariants. The Vafa–Witten invariants for b₂⁺ = 1 are related to the Donaldson invariants by the Witten conjecture, which states that the generating function of Vafa–Witten invariants is a modular form whose coefficients are the Donaldson invariants. The differential forms on the moduli space that realize this correspondence are the μ-classes μ(Σ) = ∫_Σ c₂(ad P) for Σ ∈ H₂(X, ℤ). The wall-crossing of the Vafa–Witten invariants is equivalent to the wall-crossing of the Donaldson invariants, which is given by the Kotschick–Morgan formula. The Vafa–Witten equations on a four-manifold with boundary ∂X = Y give rise to a boundary value problem where the boundary conditions are solutions of the Chern–Simons equations on Y. The differential forms on the moduli space of solutions with boundary are the relative invariants, and the gluing formula for the Vafa–Witten invariants expresses the invariants of a closed manifold as a sum over boundary conditions of products of relative invariants. The Vafa–Witten equations on a Kähler surface reduce to the Hitchin equations on the surface, and the differential forms on the moduli space are the pullbacks of the Hitchin symplectic forms. The Vafa–Witten invariants of a Kähler surface with b₂⁺ = 1 are computed by the Göttsche formula for the Betti numbers of the Hilbert scheme of points, and the wall-crossing is given by the difference of the generating functions for the two chambers. The Vafa–Witten equations with gauge group G = U(N) are related to the Donaldson–Thomas theory of the Calabi–Yau threefold X × ℂ, and the differential forms on the moduli space are the virtual fundamental classes of the moduli space of ideal sheaves. The wall-crossing for the Vafa–Witten invariants in this context is the Joyce–Song wall-crossing formula for generalized Donaldson–Thomas invariants. The quantization of the Vafa–Witten equations yields the refined Vafa–Witten invariants, which are the Poincaré polynomials of the moduli space of solutions, and the differential forms on the moduli space are the mixed Hodge structure on the cohomology. The refined wall-crossing formula is the motivic Kontsevich–Soibelman formula, and the differential forms encoding the wall-crossing are the motivic Donaldson–Thomas invariants. The Vafa–Witten equations on a four-manifold with b₂⁺ = 1 and a chosen chamber are equivalent to the Seiberg–Witten equations with a perturbation, and the differential forms on the moduli space are the Seiberg–Witten differential forms. The wall-crossing for the Vafa–Witten invariants is then the same as the wall-crossing for the Seiberg–Witten invariants, which is given by the Morgan–Mrowka–Szabó formula.

Differential Forms and the Theory of the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Local Calabi–Yau Threefolds and the Topological Vertex

Donaldson–Thomas theory on a local Calabi–Yau threefold X = Tot(K_S → S), where S is a del Pezzo surface or a toric surface, counts ideal sheaves on X with a given Chern character. The moduli space of ideal sheaves M_DT(X, β, n) carries a perfect obstruction theory, and the Donaldson–Thomas invariants DTβ,n are the integrals of the virtual fundamental class against the differential forms given by the insertions of the Chern characters of the universal sheaf. The topological vertex is a combinatorial formula for the generating function of Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the resolved conifold and its generalizations to toric Calabi–Yau threefolds. The topological vertex Vλμν is a symmetric function of three partitions λ, μ, ν, and the partition function Z_DT(X) = ∑λ,μ,ν Vλμν Vλ^t μ^t ν^t q|λ|+|μ|+|ν| … where the sum is over all triples of partitions. The differential forms on the moduli space of ideal sheaves that correspond to the topological vertex are the equivariant Chern characters of the universal sheaf with respect to the torus action on X. The localization formula expresses the Donaldson–Thomas invariants as a sum over fixed points of the torus action, and the fixed points are labeled by plane partitions (3D Young diagrams). The differential forms on the fixed point components are the equivariant Euler classes of the normal bundles, and the topological vertex computes the contribution of each fixed point. The wall-crossing for Donaldson–Thomas invariants on local Calabi–Yau threefolds is given by the Joyce–Song formula, and the differential forms encoding the wall-crossing are the motivic Donaldson–Thomas invariants. The motivic DT invariants take values in the ring of motives, and their specialization at the Lefschetz motive gives the numerical DT invariants. The topological vertex satisfies the gluing rules for the topological string partition function, and the differential forms on the moduli space of ideal sheaves on a glued threefold are the convolution of the differential forms on the pieces. The Donaldson–Thomas theory on local Calabi–Yau threefolds is equivalent to the Gromov–Witten theory of the same threefold by the MNOP conjecture (proved by Pandharipande–Pixton), and the differential forms on the DT moduli space correspond to the differential forms on the GW moduli space via the GW/DT correspondence. The topological vertex also computes the open Gromov–Witten invariants with Lagrangian branes, and the differential forms on the moduli space of open strings are the open topological string amplitudes. The quantization of the topological vertex yields the refined topological vertex, which computes the refined Donaldson–Thomas invariants (the Poincaré polynomials of the moduli space). The refined topological vertex Vλμν(q,t) depends on two parameters q and t, and the differential forms on the moduli space are the mixed Hodge polynomials. The refined wall-crossing formula is the motivic Kontsevich–Soibelman formula, and the differential forms are the motivic DT invariants. The topological vertex for the resolved conifold is related to the representation theory of the quantum group U_q(𝔰𝔩(2)) and the differential forms on the moduli space are the characters of the quantum group. The topological vertex for general toric Calabi–Yau threefolds is computed by the Aganagic–Klemm–Marino–Vafa formula using the mirror symmetry and the B-model topological string. The differential forms on the B-model moduli space are the periods of the holomorphic three-form on the mirror Calabi–Yau, and the topological vertex computes the genus-zero free energy. The higher-genus topological vertex computes the higher-genus free energy, and the differential forms on the moduli space are the higher-genus Gromov–Witten invariants. The holomorphic anomaly equation for the topological string partition function governs the dependence of the differential forms on the antiholomorphic moduli, and the topological vertex provides the boundary conditions for the holomorphic anomaly equation at the large radius limit. The Donaldson–Thomas theory on local Calabi–Yau threefolds with compact divisors is related to the stable pair theory of Pandharipande–Thomas, and the differential forms on the moduli space of stable pairs are the virtual fundamental classes of the stable pair moduli space. The wall-crossing between DT and PT invariants is given by the DT/PT correspondence formula, and the differential forms are the generating functions of the invariants.

  • Confusing the Nahm equations (1D reduction of anti-self-dual Yang–Mills) with the Hitchin equations (2D reduction); the Nahm transform maps monopoles on ℝ³ to Nahm data on an interval, while the Hitchin equations describe Higgs bundles on a Riemann surface.
  • Assuming the wild character variety is smooth; it has singularities at points where the Stokes data have non-trivial stabilizers, and the correct differential forms are the intersection cohomology forms.
  • Neglecting the role of the Langlands dual group Ǧ in the ramified Kapustin–Witten equations; the ramification data for G are nilpotent orbits in Ǧ, and S-duality exchanges G with Ǧ.
  • Forgetting that Vafa–Witten invariants for b₂⁺ = 1 depend on a chamber choice; the wall-crossing formula must be applied when comparing invariants in different chambers.
  • Confusing the topological vertex (a combinatorial formula for DT invariants of toric CY3) with the topological string partition function; the vertex is a building block, but the full partition function requires gluing rules and framing factors.
  • Assuming the Donaldson–Thomas virtual fundamental class is represented by ordinary differential forms; it is a cycle in the Chow group, and the integrals are defined via the virtual localization formula.
  • Overlooking the distinction between the wild Hitchin system (irregular singularities of order ≥2) and the tame Hitchin system (simple poles); the wild system has a cluster algebra structure and is related to Painlevé equations, while the tame system has a completely integrable structure with abelian variety fibers.

These advanced topics illustrate how differential forms on moduli spaces of solutions to gauge-theoretic equations encode deep dualities in geometry and physics, from the Nahm transform for monopoles to the topological vertex for Donaldson–Thomas theory.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Seiberg–Witten Equations on Four‑Manifolds

The Seiberg–Witten equations arise from the topological twist of N = 2 supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory on a smooth, closed, oriented four‑manifold X. They couple a U(1) connection A on a complex line bundle L to a spinor field Ψ ∈ Γ(S⁺ ⊗ L), where S⁺ is the positive spinor bundle. The equations are F_A⁺ = q(Ψ) and D_A Ψ = 0, where F_A⁺ is the self‑dual part of the curvature, q(Ψ) is a quadratic map S⁺ ⊗ L → Λ²⁺ ⊗ iℝ, and D_A is the Dirac operator twisted by A. The moduli space M_SW of solutions modulo gauge transformations is a finite‑dimensional, compact, oriented manifold of dimension d = (c₁(L)² − 2χ(X) − 3σ(X))/4, where χ is the Euler characteristic and σ the signature. Differential forms on M_SW are constructed via the universal bundle and the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for families; the Seiberg–Witten invariant SW_X(𝔰) for a spinᶜ structure 𝔰 is the evaluation of the top‑degree cohomology class μ(𝔰) ∈ H^d(M_SW) on the fundamental class. When b₂⁺(X) > 1 the invariant is independent of the metric; for b₂⁺ = 1 it jumps across walls in the space of metrics, and the wall‑crossing formula involves the intersection pairing on H²(X;ℤ). The Seiberg–Witten invariants distinguish homeomorphic but non‑diffeomorphic four‑manifolds and vanish for manifolds of positive scalar curvature. The differential forms representing μ(𝔰) are obtained by integrating the curvature of the determinant line bundle over the fibres of the universal moduli space, yielding explicit representatives in the de Rham complex of the base. These forms are central to the proof of the Thom conjecture and the adjunction inequality for embedded surfaces.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Generalized Self‑Duality Equations on Spin(7) Manifolds

On an eight‑dimensional Riemannian manifold M with holonomy contained in Spin(7), the Cayley calibration Φ ∈ Ω⁴(M) defines a class of calibrated four‑cycles. The generalized self‑duality equations for a connection A on a principal G‑bundle over M are F_A ∧ Φ = *F_A, where * is the Hodge star. Equivalently, the curvature lies in the 21‑dimensional irreducible representation of Spin(7) inside Λ² ⊗ 𝔤. The moduli space M_SD of solutions modulo gauge is expected to be a finite‑dimensional manifold whose virtual dimension is given by an index theorem involving the Pontryagin classes of the bundle and the Â‑genus of M. Differential forms on M_SD are constructed via the universal curvature and the Chern–Weil homomorphism applied to invariant polynomials on 𝔤 that are Spin(7)‑invariant. The resulting cohomology classes are integrals of characteristic forms over the fibres of the universal moduli space, producing elements in H^*(M_SD). When M is a compact Spin(7) manifold, the invariants obtained by pairing these classes with the fundamental class of M_SD are conjectured to be deformation invariants of the Spin(7) structure. For non‑compact Spin(7) manifolds asymptotic to a cone over a Sasaki–Einstein seven‑manifold, the moduli space carries a natural hyperkähler structure, and the differential forms arising from the moment maps of the gauge group action encode the geometry of the asymptotic boundary. The equations also appear in the dimensional reduction of the heterotic string on Spin(7) backgrounds, where the differential forms on the moduli space compute the superpotential and the Yukawa couplings of the effective four‑dimensional theory.

Worked Example: Computing the Virtual Dimension of the Spin(7) Instanton Moduli Space
Let M be a compact Spin(7) manifold with Â‑genus Â(M) = 1 and let E be an SU(2) bundle with c₂(E) = 5. Using the index theorem for the deformation complex of the generalized self‑duality equations, compute the virtual dimension of the moduli space M_SD.
The deformation complex is 0 → Ω⁰(ad E) → Ω¹(ad E) → Ω²21(ad E) → 0, where Ω²21 denotes the 21‑dimensional component of Λ² ⊗ ad E. Its index is ind = −∫_M Â(M) ch(ad E) |8‑form. For SU(2), ad E has rank 3 and ch(ad E) = 3 − c₂(ad E) + … . Since c₂(ad E) = 4c₂(E) = 20, the degree‑8 part of Â(M) ch(ad E) is Â(M) ⋅ (−c₂(ad E)) = −20. Thus ind = −(−20) = 20. The virtual dimension of M_SD is 20.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the virtual dimension of a moduli space defined by a first‑order elliptic system, identify the deformation complex, write its symbol sequence, and apply the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for families. Memorise the standard index formulas for anti‑self‑dual Yang–Mills (4k − 3(1−b₁+b₂⁺)), Seiberg–Witten ((c₁²−2χ−3σ)/4), and Spin(7) instantons (−∫ Â ch(ad E)). Recognise that the Chern character of the adjoint bundle for SU(n) is n − 2n c₂(E) + … . Practice extracting the degree‑8 component quickly.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Kapustin–Witten Equations on Manifolds with Corners

The Kapustin–Witten equations are a one‑parameter family of gauge‑theoretic equations on a four‑manifold X that interpolate between the anti‑self‑dual Yang–Mills equations (t = 0) and the Hitchin equations on a Riemann surface (t → ∞). When X is a manifold with corners of codimension up to two, the equations require boundary conditions of Nahm‑pole type on each boundary hypersurface and compatibility conditions at the corners. The moduli space M_KW(t) of solutions modulo gauge transformations is a stratified space whose top stratum is a smooth manifold of dimension given by an index theorem with boundary contributions from the η‑invariant of the induced operator on the boundary. Differential forms on M_KW(t) are constructed using the relative de Rham complex of the pair (X, ∂X) and the universal bundle over the moduli space. The corner contributions appear as integrals of Chern–Simons forms over the codimension‑two strata, and they ensure that the Stokes theorem holds for the differential forms representing the characteristic classes of the universal bundle. These forms are essential for defining the topological invariants of the Kapustin–Witten theory on manifolds with corners, which are conjectured to compute the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of links in the three‑sphere via the geometric Langlands correspondence. The wall‑crossing behaviour as t varies is governed by the appearance of reducible solutions at the corners, and the differential forms encode the jump in the invariants through the residue of the η‑invariant at the critical values of t.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Calabi–Yau Fourfolds

Donaldson–Thomas theory on a Calabi–Yau fourfold Y counts ideal sheaves of subschemes of dimension two (or one) with a virtual fundamental class constructed via the obstruction theory of the moduli stack. The moduli space M_DT of stable objects in the derived category D^b(Coh(Y)) carries a symmetric obstruction theory of virtual dimension zero. The virtual fundamental class [M_DT]^vir ∈ A0(M_DT) is defined using the Behrend–Fantechi construction, and its degree gives the Donaldson–Thomas invariant DT(Y, β) for a curve class β ∈ H₄(Y;ℤ). Differential forms on M_DT arise from the Chern character of the universal complex and the Todd class of Y, integrated over the virtual fundamental class via the virtual localisation formula when a torus action is present. The generating function of DT invariants is conjectured to equal the topological string partition function on Y, and the differential forms on M_DT correspond to the B‑model amplitudes computed by the holomorphic anomaly equations. For local Calabi–Yau fourfolds given by the total space of a canonical bundle over a Fano threefold, the DT invariants are related to the Gromov–Witten invariants of the base by a higher‑dimensional MNOP‑type correspondence. The differential forms representing the virtual class are not ordinary forms but cycles in the Chow group; their integrals are defined by the virtual localisation formula of Graber–Pandharipande. The wall‑crossing between different stability conditions is governed by the Joyce–Song wall‑crossing formula, and the differential forms on the moduli space of semistable objects are the motivic DT invariants taking values in the Grothendieck ring of varieties.

Differential Forms and the Geometry of the Moduli Space of Solutions to the Vafa–Witten Equations on Four‑Manifolds with b₂⁺ = 0

The Vafa–Witten equations are the topological twist of N = 4 super‑Yang–Mills theory on a closed oriented four‑manifold X. They involve a connection A on a principal G‑bundle, a self‑dual two‑form B ∈ Ω²⁺(ad P), and a scalar φ ∈ Ω⁰(ad P). The equations are F_A⁺ = [B, B] + d_A φ, d_A^* B = 0, and d_A^* d_A φ = 0. When b₂⁺(X) = 0, the moduli space M_VW is compact and its virtual dimension is zero. The Vafa–Witten invariant VW_X(G) is the Euler characteristic of the virtual fundamental class, which can be computed by localisation with respect to the U(1) action rotating B and φ. Differential forms on M_VW are constructed from the universal curvature and the equivariant Chern–Weil forms; the invariant is the integral of the equivariant Euler class of the obstruction bundle over the fixed‑point locus. For G = SU(2) and X a negative‑definite four‑manifold, the invariants are expressed in terms of the Seiberg–Witten basic classes of X via the Vafa–Witten formula VW_X = Σκ SW_X(κ) qκ²/4, where the sum runs over spinᶜ structures with non‑zero Seiberg–Witten invariant. The differential forms representing the equivariant Euler class are polynomials in the curvature of the universal bundle and the equivariant parameter; their integrals localise to contributions from the fixed points, which are abelian instantons. The wall‑crossing for b₂⁺ = 1 is absent because the moduli space is already compact, but the invariants depend on the choice of a chamber in the space of metrics when b₂⁺ > 0. The Vafa–Witten invariants are conjectured to be the Fourier coefficients of a mock modular form, and the differential forms on the moduli space provide the geometric realisation of the holomorphic anomaly that governs the modular completion.

  • Confusing the virtual dimension formula for Spin(7) instantons with that for G₂ instantons; the Spin(7) index involves the 21‑dimensional representation, while G₂ uses the 14‑dimensional one.
